PREFACE 

The lessons of this text have been prepared in the conviction that the primary purpose of an elementary lesson book in German is to acquaint the student with the essential and fundamental facts of German grammar. This conviction has determined the choice of vocabulary, the subject matter of the exercises, and the presentation of grammatical material. 

Grammar has not been conceived of as an end in itself, but rather as a means of reducing the complexities of inflection and syntax to ordered simplicity as the multiplication table is a device for the simplification of tedious arithmetical processes. Without a reasonable mastery of German grammar it is illusory to expect any real achievement in knowledge and use of the language: one may sputter words, but there is no intelligent expression of ideas; one may know vaguely what a piece of prose or poetry is about, but its real meaning is seen only as through a glass, darkly; without a substantial knowledge of grammar a reading knowledge for purposes of scientific study is impossible. Ten years of experience with the special examinations for elementary and reading knowledge of German in Harvard University have driven home to me with ever recurring force the fact that the one possible short cut and the nearest approach to a "royal road" in acquiring a reading knowledge of German is a thorough mastery of the inflections and the essential syntax of the language. The lessons of this text have been planned to guide the student as expeditiously as possible to this mastery of essentials.

No attempt has been made to conceal the fact that a living language cannot be cast into forms of absolute iii

iv 

FUNDAMENTALS OF GERMAN 

rigidity. It has, therefore, frequently been necessary to use the unfortunately vague words commonly, usually, generally, where the earnest student craves the positive always. However, positive and definite rules play such a large part in German grammar, even in the declension of nouns, that the student who gains a firm hold upon a broad generality has won a fixed point about which he may group with certainty many divergent and troublesome details and exceptions.

The vocabulary and constructions used in the exercises of the text are normal German, such as is used by any German in every-day life. A group of Oral Exercises based on Exercise A of each lesson is given on pages 163178. The experienced teacher knows the value of such exercises in developing coördination of eye, ear, and tongue, so fundamental in the study of a foreign language. 

The lessons as laid out in the text are planned for mature students with a background of foreign-language study. For classes of less experience and maturity, however, the teacher may make a material readjustment of lessons. Each lesson may well be broken up into three parts: (1) Grammar and Exercise A, (2) Oral Exercises, 

(3) Exercise B. Exercise A, which is the nucleus about which each lesson is built, should, before assignment, be read aloud by the teacher, sentence by sentence, and repeated viva voce and in unison by the class. 

I wish to express my sense of obligation for the helpful interest of colleagues as the work of the text went forward. 

I am indebted to Doctor G. K. Zipf and to Professors F. S. 

Cawley and F. W. C. Lieder for constructive criticism and valuable suggestions before the manuscript went to press. 

To Ginn and Company I am indebted for the courtesy and care with which they looked after all details of the publication of the work. Doctor M. F. Schmits of Carleton College very kindly aided me in the reading of proof.

CAMBRIDGE, MASSACHUSETTS G. M. H.

Charlottenburg, d. 2. Ott. 1933

Liebe Frau Doktor! 

Ich habe mich sehr gefreut, den freundlichen Brief zu erhalten, den Sie durch Ihren lieben 

Jungen an mich gesandt haben. Es macht mir große Freude, daß die Guttmanns zu Nachbare haben sollen. Wenn Sie einander kennenlernen so werden Sie sich gewiß bald sehr liebgewinnen. 

Es wäre mir sehr lieb, wenn Sie übermorgen. um etwa wier Uhr zu mir kommen könnten, denn Frau Guttmann wird auch zu der Zeit bei mir sein, und wir können dann eine 

Tasse Kaffen zusammen trinken. Wenn ich gewüßt hätte, daß Sie und Guttmanns sich nicht kaunten, so hätte ich die gewiß schon frücher zusammengebracht. Ich und Frau Guttmann waren als junge Mädchen in der Schule zusammen, und mir ist es oft, als wären wir Schwestern, so lieb habe ich fin. Hätte sie nur nicht so viel Unglück gehabt! er wollte.

Lassen Sie die Kinder zusammen sigielen, so oft sie nur mögen. Ich muß jetzt diesem 

Brieschen ein Ende machen. Karl wartet noch bei meinem Fritzehen; faryta, meine Antwort mit nach Hause bringen, wenn ich die Zeit hätte, gleich zu schreiben. Er ist ein lieber Junge. In der Hoffnung 

Sie übermorgen bei mir zu sehen, liebe Frau- 

Dottor, verbleibe ich yar Ihre 

Dorothea König

PRONUNCIATION 

Vowels

German long vowels require more time for their pronunciation than short ones. A similar difference in the length of vowels is frequently made in English: the a of father, for example, is given more time than the a of what; the o of rode is given more time than the o of rope. 

A a long, like a in father: Vater father. 

A a short, like a in what: was what. 

Ge long, like a in made: geben (the root vowel) give. 

Ge short, like e in met: Ende (the root vowel) end. e final, in unaccented endings (-el, en, etc.) and in the prefixes be, ge, like the English unaccented e in father or the final a of drama 

Ende (the final e) end ſehen (e of the last syllable) see beginnen (e of the prefix) begin i long, like ee in see: ihm him. 

Ji short, like i in pit: in in. long, like o in rode: rot red. short, like o in official: hoffen hope. 

U u long, like u in rude: Hut hat. 

I u short, like u in put: Mutter mother.

Yas a vowel, long and short, like German i long and short or like it long and short.

Ai ai (ay), like i in mine. 

Kaiser emperor

Diphthongs 

Bayern Bavaria 

Au au, like ou in house: Haus house. 

Au äu, like oy in toy: Häuſer houses.

Ei ei (ey), like i in mine. flein small 

Eu eu, like oy in toy: neun nine. 

Meyer a proper noun ie (in reality long i) like ee in see: dies this. 

Modified Vowels 

Ää long, like ai in failure: Väter fathers.

Ää short, like e in met or German e short in Ende: Bänke benches. long and short and ü ü long and short are pronounced with closely rounded lips. The correct lip position may be obtained by resting the blunt end of a pencil upon the lower lip and closing the lips gently about it. With the lips in this position pronounce long like a in made: Öfen stoves. short like e in met: öffnen open. 

Üü long like ee in see: Güte goodness. 

Üü short like i in hit: Mütter mothers. 

Äu äu like oy in toy or German eu: Häuser houses.

Consonants 

Bb at the end of a word or syllable and before voiceless consonants, like p in cap: Grab grave; elsewhere like b in boy: beißen bite.

Cc before back vowels (a, o, u, au) or a consonant, like c in cave: Café café; elsewhere like ts in hats: Cäsar Cæsar. The character occurs only in words of foreign origin. 

Ch ch after a, o, u, or au, like ch in the Scotch word loch, a voiceless guttural sound produced by the friction of the breath passing between the back of the palate and the tongue (guttural ch): Buch book; after all other letters and

INTRODUCTION 

7 always in the suffix chen, like a very long whispered h of the word hew (palatal ch): ich I. The correct palatal sound will be obtained by inserting the end of a match about half an inch between the teeth, closing the teeth gently upon it, and whispering slowly the word hew. To pronounce the palatal ch correctly the tip of the tongue must touch gently the back of the lower teeth. In words of Greek or Latin origin, when initial or before a, o, or a consonant, like English k (usually): Christ Christian. In French words, like English sh: Chef chef. In other words of foreign origin ch is usually pronounced like the whispered h of hew (palatal ch): Chemie chemistry. chs or chs like x in box: Ochs ox. df like k in bake: backen bake.

Gg at the beginning of a word or syllable, like g in go: gehen go. g at the end of a word or syllable: In the ending -ig it is always like the palatal ch, so that the word König king is pronounced as if it were written Könich. Under all other circumstances (except after n) there are two possible pronunciations, both widely accepted: 

1. After consonants and after front vowels,² like German palatal ch or like f; Berg mountain and Krieg war are, therefore, pronounced as if written Berch, Kriech, or 

Berk, Kriek. (But see ng below.) 

2. After back vowels,2 like German guttural d or like f; 

Tag day is, therefore, pronounced as if written Tach or Taf. (But see ng below.)

2 Front vowels are e, i, ä, ö, ü, ai, ei, ie, äu, eu. Back vowels are a, o, u, au.

Gg medial has also two possible pronounciations: 

1. Like ch (palatal after front vowels and consonants except n; guttural after back vowels), but accompanied by vibration of the vocal cords. 

2. Like English hard g in ago.

One who pronounces final g like ch will pronounce medial g like ch voiced; one who pronounces final g like f will pronounce medial g like English hard g.

Accentuation 

German words are regularly accented on the first syllable: las'sen let.

Compound words regularly have the main accent on the first syllable of the first word, with a secondary accent on the first syllable of the second word: ein'las'sen let in

Wohn'haus' dwelling-house 

The prefixes be-, emp-, ent-, er-, ge-, ver-, zer-, are not accented; the accent usually falls on the syllable following the prefix: 

verlassen leave, desert 

Gedan'te thought

There are many exceptions (most of them only apparent exceptions) to the general rules given above, and accents (not used in German printing) will be frequently used in the vocabularies of this text in order to indicate the correct accentuation of words.

Quantity of Vowels 

Double vowels and diphthongs are long: 

See lake, sea 

Haus house

An accented vowel followed by one consonant is regularly long: Vater father.

Vowels followed by h before either another vowel or a consonant are long: gehen go nehmen take An accented vowel followed by two or more consonants is usually short: Mutter mother.

A regular exception to the rules given above is that a long vowel of a verbal stem is not shortened by the suffixing of inflectional endings: leben live er lebt he lives

11 In the prefixes be-, emp-, ent-, er, ge-, ver-, zer-, e is short.

Vowels before ch and B are sometimes long, sometimes short:

Buch book sprechen speak

Grüß greeting daß that The length of vowels will occasionally be indicated in the vocabularies and paradigms of this text, though they are not indicated in German printing.

GENERAL RULES TO BE OBSERVED IN WRITING GERMAN EXERCISES Capital Letters 

Rules governing the use of capital letters are essentially the same in German as in English. Note, however, the 

Nouns begin with capital letters: mein Bruder my brother. 

Adjectives used as nouns begin with capital letters: etwas Gutes something good alt (adjective) old der Alte (noun) the old man 

Adjectives derived from proper nouns are regularly written with small letters: die amerikanischen Zeitungen the American newspapers.

Adjectives derived from proper nouns by means of the suffixes -er and ſch are written with capital letters: die Berliner Zeitungen the Berlin newspapers 

Schillersch of Schiller

The pronoun ich I begins with a small letter.

Punctuation 

German punctuation is, generally speaking, very similar to that which prevails in English. Note, however, the following rules: 

Do not use a comma before und and used to connect the last two members of a series of words: 

Er kam in einem Automobile mit Frau, Kindern und Hunden.

He came in an automobile with wife, children, and dogs. 

Do not use a comma before und and connecting two main clauses that have one subject:

Er fand das Wetter heiß und blieb nicht lange da. He found the weather hot, and did not stay there long. 

In English too this comma is frequently omitted. 

Dependent clauses are set off with commas:

Das Buch, das mein Bruder mir gab, ist nicht sehr intereſſant. 

The book that my brother gave me is not very interesting.

Dependent infinitive constructions are set off with commas when the infinitive is accompanied by a modifier; otherwise no comma is used: But 

Er bat meinen Bruder, morgen zu kommen. He asked my brother to come tomorrow.

Er bat meinen Bruder zu kommen. He asked my brother to come. 

Do not set off with a comma an adverbial phrase at the beginning of a sentence:

Zur Zeit Friedrichs des Großen war Kant der größte Philosoph der Welt. At the time of Frederick the Great, Kant was the greatest philosopher of the world.

In English too this comma is frequently omitted.

13 

The Division of Words at the End of a Line 

A single consonant between vowels is carried over to the next line: Bru-der brother.

Two or more consonants between vowels are so divided that only one consonant is carried over to the next line : 

Mut-ter mother fämp-fen fight (But see the characters dh, ſch, etc. below.)

Compound words are divided into their component parts: Wohn-haus dwelling-house. 

ď becomes f-f: backen bake when divided becomes baf-fen. 

The characters ch, sch, ß, ph, th, and ſt are not divided. 

Note the following divisions: 

Büscher books 

So-phie Sophy ach-ten esteem ha-schen seize 

Füße feet ta-tholisch Catholic 

Fen-ster window be-ſte best

Use of Roman Script 

For the German character ß use preferably ss; ß may be used; sz is not desirable. 

Instead of the German double hyphen - use the single hyphen -. 

SIMILARITIES BETWEEN GERMAN AND ENGLISH 

The many striking resemblances between German and English that the English-speaking student of German will very soon discover are readily explained by certain facts of English history.

The inhabitants of England at the time of Julius Cæsar were Celts, who spoke a language very different from English or German, though distantly related to them both.

About the middle of the fifth century the Celts of Britannia, as the Romans then called England, asked certain Germanic tribes living along the coast of the North Sea to come to their aid against the Picts and Scots of southern Scotland. The Germanic peoples came in great numbers, founded kingdoms of their own in the country, and drove the Celts partly into the western and southwestern portions of the island, partly across the Channel into northwestern France. Thus a variety of Germanic dialects became the basis of the later English language. 

The dialects of both Germany and England have, of course, undergone great changes since the Germanic tribes first settled in England; one of the greatest changes that 

English has undergone was due to the conquest of England by William the Conqueror in 1066. But notwithstanding the changes that have taken place in both German and English throughout the centuries, the kinship between the two languages is very evident.

The most frequent and regular sound relationships between English and German will be set forth in the following paragraphs; the student will find help in the acquisition of a German vocabulary by noting these resemblances. 

He must realize, however, that many words have changed their meanings as well as their sounds during the centuries that have passed, and should not feel that cognate words¹ in modern English and German are necessarily exact equivalents of each other in meaning.

Illustrations of sound changes given below are limited to words in which the German and English meanings are practically identical, in order that the English word that is given may serve at once as an illustration of the sound 1 By cognate words we mean words derived from a form or root common to both English and German.

15 relationship and as a definition of the German word. It would be equally easy to present cognates different in meaning.

1. 

LESSON I Verbs 

Comparatively speaking, the German verb system is easy; it offers no more difficulties than the English does to a foreigner. As in English, the mastery of German verb forms requires the learning of a few fundamental rules and the memorizing of typical regular forms and of certain irregular ones. In both languages the verb be shows several irregularities. 2. ich bin I am

Present Indicative of ſein be du bist you are (thou art) er (fie, es) ist he (she, it) is wir sind we are ihr seid you (ye) are fie find they are 

Sie find you are 

3. Of the three words meaning you, du and ihr are used in addressing children, near relatives, and intimate friends, du for the singular, ihr for the plural. Otherwise Sie is used for both singular and plural. a. In his general reading the student will meet exceptions to the statement made above: du is frequently found in popular stories where Sie would seem to be called for; Jhr, written with a capital, was once widely used in addressing one person, where now Sie is used. In epistolary style the familiar du is written 

Du, with a capital; du is used in worship. 4. 

Models of the Present Indicative fra'gen ask ich frage I ask du fragst you ask er fragt he asks ant'worten answer ich antworte I answer du antwortest you answer er antwortet he answers 19

[5 

wir fragen we ask wir antworten we answer ihr antwortet you answer ſie antworten they answer 

Sie antworten you answer ihr fragt you ask fie fragen they ask 

Sie fragen you ask a. The stem of a verb is found by dropping the -en, occasionally only n, of the infinitive. In the inflection of verbs the personal endings, indicated above for the present tense by heavy-faced type, are added to the stem. b. Verbs whose stem ends in -d, -t, or two consonants after which ſt and t cannot be readily pronounced, regularly have an e between the stem and the personal endings =ſt and -t: du antwortest er antwortet ihr antwortet c. Verbs whose stem ends in a sibilant, -8, -ſſ, -ß, -ſch, 13, x, -3, usually contract the form of the second personal singular by dropping the f of the personal ending. The verb forms of the second and third person singular are then identical. du sitt you sit er sitt he sits Occasionally, however, stems ending in a sibilant add an e before the ending of the second person singular instead of contracting the form: du ſiteſt you sit. d. Verbs with stems ending in -el and -er have only n in the infinitive, wan'dern wander, and usually drop the e of the stem-ending -el or er in the first person singular: ich mandre 

I wander. e. The English language has a variety of verb forms for each person and tense; these are expressed by only one form in German. For example, in the present tense: I go, I am going, I do go ich gehe. 5. 

Gender, Number, and Case 

German has three genders, masculine, feminine, and neuter; two numbers, singular and plural; four cases, nominative, genitive, dative, and accusative.

6, 7] 

LESSON I 

1. Grammatical gender in German is sometimes different from natural gender: das Mädchen the girl. 

2. The nominative case is used in German essentially as it is in English; the genitive case is used to express possession, and as the equivalent of many English prepositional expressions introduced by of; the dative case is commonly used to express the idea to or from; the accusative case, as in English, is the case of the direct object. 

The three oblique cases, genitive, dative, and accusative, are frequently governed by verbs, prepositions, and adjectives, offering thus a variety of dependent case forms for the one objective case of the English. 6.

The definite article is used very frequently in German where the English idiom requires the use of a possessive adjective. See Exercise A, sentences 2, 4, 6, 7, 16, 17, of this lesson. 7. 

Declension of Nouns 

1. German nouns fall naturally into five declensional classes, which are distinguished primarily by the form of the noun in the plural. The student should learn with the

[8 

definite article the nominative singular, genitive singular, and nominative plural of each noun in the vocabularies: der Vater des Vaters die Väter das Mädchen des Mädchens die Mädchen 

2. The following general rules apply to nouns of all 

a. Feminine nouns are uninflected in the singular. b. The dative plural of all nouns ends in n; all other cases of the plural are alike. 8. 1.

LESSON I 

a. The use of the umlaut is restricted to the letters a, o, u, and au, but there is no certain rule governing its use in forming the plural of nouns of Class I. The more common masculine nouns have it; the two feminines, Mutter and Tochter, have it; only one neuter has it as a sign of the plural: das Klō'ster, die Klöster monastery, convent. 

b.

Proper names usually form the genitive singular by adding 8: 

Frau Kellers Garten Mrs. Keller's garden 

Karls Vater Charles's father

Those ending in a sibilant add an apostrophe or -ens: Fritz' or 

Frizens Vater. c. Family names, in ordinary usage, form the plural with 8: 

Schmidts the Smiths. 

9. Words ending in chen and lein are neuter, irrespective of the natural gender of the persons or animals they indicate. 10.

Position of Adverbs 

1. Interrogative adverbs stand first in a question: 

Wo ist der Vater? Where is Father? 

2. Other adverbs and adverbial expressions usually stand in the following relative positions in a sentence: time first, place last, other adverbial modifiers between those of time and place: 

Die Mutter ist jetzt (1) mit Fräulein Schmidt (2) in dem

Garten (3). The mother is now in the garden with Miss Smith. 

3. Of two adverbs of place, the more specific will usually stand last: 

Karl ſitt im Zimmer am Fenster. Charles is sitting by the window in the room.

EXERCISE A 

1. Karl ſitt im Zimmer am Fenster. 2. Fräulein Schmidt kommt ins Zimmer und fragt: „Ist der Vater zu Hause, Karl?" 

3. Karl antwortet: „Ja, Fräulein Schmidt, er ist im Garten." 

4. Wo ist die Mutter? 5. Die Mutter ist mit den Mädchen im 

Garten. 6. Und wo sind die Brüder? 7. Die Brüder sind mit

LESSON I 

25 dem Vater im Garten. 8. Die Mutter kommt jetzt mit den 

Töchtern aus dem Garten. 9. Sie sagt: „Guten Morgen, Fräulein Schmidt." 10. Fräulein Schmidt antwortet: „Guten 

Morgen, Frau Keller." 11. Fräulein Schmidt und die Mutter gehen jetzt in den Garten. 12. Die Mädchen gehen zu Karl und fragen: „Kommst du auch nicht in den Garten?" 13. Karl antwortet: „Ja, ich gehe jetzt auch in den Garten." 14. Er geht aus dem Zimmer. 15. Die Mädchen gehen auch aus dem Zimmer. 

16. Die Töchter sind jetzt mit der Mutter und dem Fräulein in dem Garten. 17. Karl und die Brüder sind mit dem Vater in dem Garten.

EXERCISE B 

1. Are you coming into the garden with Miss Smith, Charles? 

2. No, Father, I am not going into the garden. 3. The young lady asks, "Charles, where are the girls?" 4. I answer, "The girls are with their brothers by the window." 5. Where are you, Mother? 6. I am sitting with Charles by the window. 7. Are you sitting in the room, Charles, or are you in the garden? 8. I am now in the garden. 9. Charles, are the girls also going into the garden with their mother? 10. Yes, they are also going with (the) Father and (the) Mother into the garden. 11. With whom are you going into the room, Miss Keller? 12. I am going into the room with Mrs. Smith's daughters. 13. I ask my mother, "Where are you sitting now, Mother?" 14. My mother answers, "I am sitting in the garden with Miss Smith." 

15. Are the young ladies now in the garden or in the room? 

16. They are also sitting in the garden now. 17. What are you saying, Miss Smith? 18. I say, "The girls are not with their brothers in the room." 19. What does your brother say, Charles? 

20. He says, "The girls are now at home." 21. Are you asking (the) Father or (the) Mother, Miss Smith? 22. I am asking your mother, Charles; your father is not at home.

Principal Parts of Verbs VERBS WEAK AND STRONG 

1. In both English and German a verb has three forms from which all other forms are derived: the infinitive, the first person singular of the past indicative, and the past participle. These forms are called the principal parts. 

German verbs corresponding to English regular verbs are called Weak Verbs; those corresponding to English irregular verbs are called Strong Verbs.

INFINITIVE 

fra'gen ask

PAST ant'wortete answered ant'worten answer frag'te asked ſa'gen say fag'te said fom'men come fam came

PAST PARTICIPLE 

gefragt' asked geant'wortet answered gefagt' said gekom'men come a. The present participle, which is formed by adding to the infinitive, is of minor importance, being used mainly as an adjective. 

2. The student should learn the principal parts of every strong verb as soon as he meets it; there is no other factor in building a vocabulary that gives so large a return for so little labor. 26

Nouns of Class II NOUNS BELONGING TO CLASS II 

a. Most masculine nouns of more than one syllable. (Exceptions to this general rule are the masculine nouns ending in -el, -en, -er, which belong to Class I, masculine nouns ending in -e, which belong almost entirely to Class IV, and a few others that will be treated specifically later.) b. Most masculine nouns of one syllable. c. A limited number of feminine nouns of one syllable and a few ending in -nis and ſal.

15] 

LESSON II 

d. Many neuter nouns of one syllable, and a limited number of more than one syllable more specifically, those ending in -nis and ſal and some beginning with Ge- but not ending in -e. (Neuters ending in -el, en, er, chen, and -lein and those beginning with Ge- and ending in -e are in Class I.) e. Class II, therefore, contains the great majority of all monosyllabic nouns and the majority of masculine polysyllabics. 2.

Class II always has the full ending -es if the word ends in 8, ß, ſch, t, or 3, and usually also if it ends in 3t or st. Monosyllabics ending in a double consonant usually have the full ending -es. b. There is a tendency to use the full genitive ending -es and the dative ending -e with masculine and neuter monosyllabics, and to omit the e in both cases with polysyllabics, except as indicated under above; but more often the use or the omission of the e is determined by sentence rhythm or by the sounds following. c. Feminine monosyllabics of Class II always have umlaut in the plural; masculine monosyllabics usually have it; neuter monosyllabics never have it.

[16 

d. Masculine polysyllabics derived from verbs usually have umlaut in the plural; other masculine polysyllabics and neuter polysyllabics do not. e. -nis changes to -niſſ- before the inflectional ending e: das Gefäng'nis prison des Gefängnisses die Gefängnisse 

16. Word Order: Normal and Inverted Order 

1. In simple declarative sentences and in direct questions the verb occupies the second place. 

2. A sentence in which the subject (sometimes with modifiers) precedes the verb is said to have Normal Order: 

Fräulein Schmidt ist mit den Mädchen im Garten. 

3. A sentence in which the verb precedes the subject is said to have Inverted Order. a. An adverb or an object placed at the beginning of a declarative sentence, frequently to emphasize the adverb or object, is by far the most frequent reason for inverted order: 

Jetzt ist er im Garten. Now he is in the garden. 

Ihren Bruder sehe ich nicht. I do not see your brother (although 

I do see someone else). b. Such a sentence as Ja, Vater, der Bruder ist in dem Garten has normal order, since the declarative sentence begins with its subject, der Bruder. c. A coördinating conjunction does not affect the word order in any way, since it is used only to connect sentences or clauses and is not an integral part of them:

Der Vater ist in dem Zimmer, und die Mutter ist in dem 

Garten. 

The coördinating conjunctions in German are a'ber but allein' but (still, nevertheless) denn for o'der or fon'dern but (on the contrary) und and

EXERCISE B 

1. My mother's friends have two daughters. 2. At eight o'clock our sons drove to Rosenberg. 3. Have you a carriage, Mrs. Keller? 4. No, we have no carriage, but my brothers in the country have two carriages. 5. Your daughters are very beautiful, Mrs. Smith; have you sons also? 6. Yes, Miss Keller, I have also two sons. 7. Girls, where are your brothers? 8. Our brothers are in the country with their father. 9. Then we said good night, and went home. 10. I did not find your sons at home, Mrs. Smith, but [I did find] your daughters. 11. At nine o'clock the day was very beautiful. 12. Are your sons at home, Mrs. Keller? 13. Yes, Charles; you [will] find my sons in the garden with their father, but the girls are at Miss Smith's.

14. My brothers said good night, but I remained in the garden with mother and the young lady. 15. The girl and her brothers came into the room again and said good night. 18. Before nightfall we found our carriage again; then we drove home.

LESSON III Inseparable Prefixes 

Verbs compounded with the inseparable prefixes be-, emp-, ent-, er-, ge-, ver-, and zer- form the past participle without ge; when they are used with the sign of the infinitive zu to, this zu precedes the verb. Compounds with these prefixes have the accent of the stem of the verb. 

Principal parts: besu'chen, besuch'te, besucht' visit, call on. 

Infinitive with zu: Ich hoffe, ihn heute zu besuchen. I hope to call on him today. 18.

LESSON III 

a. The forms mein, dein, and ſein occur occasionally instead of the longer forms given above for the genitive of the first and second person singular, and the genitive of the masculine and neuter singular of the third person. b. The distinction between the pronouns of the second person, du, ihr, Sie, is explained in article 3. c. Pronouns agree with their antecedents in gender and number. 19. 1. 

Nouns of Class III NOUNS BELONGING TO CLASS III 

a. A limited number of masculine monosyllabic nouns, and only two of frequent occurrence having more than one syllable. der Reich'tum wealth, riches der Frr'tum error

All other words ending in -tum are neuter. b. Many common neuter monosyllabics; neuters ending in -tum; a few neuters beginning with Ge-, but not ending in -e. (Neuters beginning with Ge- and not ending in -e are divided, therefore, between Class II and Class III.) c. No feminine nouns. 

2. house

MODELS OF CLASS III 

man, husband

LESSON IV 21. Models of the Present Perfect Indicative 

I have read, I have been reading, etc. 1. ich habe gelesen wir haben gelesen du hast gelesen ihr habt gelesen er hat gelesen ſie haben gelesen 

Sie haben gelesen 2. 

I have come etc. ich bin gekommen wir sind gekommen du bist gekommen ihr seid gekommen er ist gekommen sie sind gekommen 

Sie sind gekommen 3. The present perfect tense of a verb is formed by adding the past participle of the verb to the present tense of haben or sein. a. Haben is used to form the present perfect tense of all transitive verbs, and of intransitive verbs expressing mere action without change of position or change of condition: 

Ich habe Ihren Freund besucht. I have visited your friend. 

(A transitive verb) 

Ich habe in dem Garten gearbeitet. I have been working in the garden. (An intransitive verb expressing neither change of position nor change of condition) b. Sein is used to form the present perfect tense of intransitive verbs expressing change of position or change of condition, and of begegnen meet, bleiben remain, gelingen succeed, geschehen happen, ſein be, and werden become. 

Ich bin nach Hause gegangen. I went home. (An intransitive verb expressing change of position) 

Mein Hund ist gestern gestorben. My dog died yesterday. (An intransitive verb expressing change of condition) 39

[22 

In the vocabularies of this text the past participles of verbs forming the perfect tenses by means of the auxiliary ſein are preceded by iſt kommen, kam, ist gekommen come. c. The perfect infinitive forms, such as gelesen haben, gegangen ſein, are used in forming future perfect and perfect conditional tenses. The use of the future perfect tense in German is rare, so that the main use of the perfect infinitive is in the perfect conditional forms. 22. 

Tenses: Past and Present Perfect 

As the equivalent of the English past tense the German uses sometimes the past tense, sometimes the present perfect. The following rules present the prevailing usage: 

1. The past tense is used to express a. Continued action in past time:

Wir fuhren durch den Wald. We drove (were driving) through the forest. b. Customary or repeated action in past time:

Wir gingen um acht Uhr in die Schule. We went to school at eight o'clock (this was our usual practice). c. A series of individual but related or consecutive actions in past time:

Dann fuhren wir über die Felder. Herr Werner redete ein paar 

Worte mit den Männern über die Felder und das Wetter. 

Nun führte uns der Weg durch Bäume und Wälder. (See 

Exercise A, Lesson III.) 

2. To express action in past time in other relations than those above indicated, the German regularly uses the present perfect tense: 

In meiner Klaſſe haben wir ein Gedicht von Schiller gelesen. In my class we read a poem of Schiller's.

23, 24] 41 

LESSON IV 

a. In the translation of English into German, the German present perfect will be the usual form for the English present perfect: 

Haben Sie Schillers Gedichte gelesen? Ja, ich habe sie gelesen. 

Have you read Schiller's poems? Yes, I have read them. 

Word Order: Dependent Participles 

In a simple declarative sentence or a direct question, a dependent participle stands at the end of the sentence: 

Ich habe mit meinem Vater im Garten gearbeitet. I worked in the garden with my father. 24. 1.

Formation of the Past Perfect Tense 

The past perfect tense of a verb is formed by adding its past participle to the past tense of haben or ſein in accordance with the rules given in 21, 3, a, b. For the position of the dependent participle see 23.

Ich hatte meinen Freund in der Stadt besucht. I had visited my friend in the city.

Wir waren durch den Wald gefahren. We had driven through the forest. 27. 

Pronominal Adjectives 

Pronominal adjectives are words that are used sometimes as adjectives, to modify nouns, sometimes as pronouns, that is, in place of nouns :

Dieser Freund wohnt auf dem Lande, dieser wohnt in der Stadt. 

This friend lives in the country; this one lives in the city. 

The first dieser is an adjective; the second one is a pronoun. 1. 

DECLENSION OF die'ſer this, this one, the latter 

SINGULAR FEM. 

PLURAL MASC. NEUT. 

ALL GENDERS 

NOм. dieser diese dieſes diese 

GEN. dieses dieser dieses dieser DAT. dieſem dieser diesem diesen Acc. diesen diese dieses diese 45

[27 

2. The following pronominal adjectives, subject to the deviations indicated, are declined like dieſer : all- all je'der each, each one, every je'ner that, that one, the former manch- many, many a (one) folch such, such a viel- many, much, a great deal (of) welch who, which, that wenig few, little a. all may be uninflected before other pronominal adjectives and before possessive adjectives: all meine Bücher all my books. b. jeder is inflected in the singular only. c. manch, solch-, and welch are frequently uninflected before ein and before adjectives:

Solch ein Bild ist sehr schön. Such a picture is very beautiful. 

Solch schöne Bücher. Such beautiful books. d. viel in the sense of much is usually uninflected: 

Er gab mir viel Geld. He gave me much money. e. wenig in the sense of little is frequently uninflected: 

Er hatte wenig Geld. He had little money. 

3. The following pronominal adjectives are inflected like dieser, but are used for the most part only in the plural: an'dere others bei'de both ei'nige some et'liche some (infrequent) meh'rere several 4. ein and fein, when used as pronouns, are declined like dieser : 

Einer von meinen Brüdern war nicht zu Hause. One of my brothers was not at home.

Keins von den Kindern war zu Hause. Not one of the children was at home. a. In the nominative and accusative singular of the neuter, ein and fein usually have the contracted form eins and feins. 

5. The pronominal adjectives ein paar a few, etwas some, and several others are not inflected.

28, 29] 47 

LESSON V 

Word Order: Transposed Order 

A dependent clause introduced by a subordinating conjunction, by a relative pronoun, or by an interrogative pronoun or adverb has the Transposed Order; that is, the verb is placed at the end of the subordinate clause : 

Ich glaubte nicht, daß du so viel Geld hattest. I did not think that you had so much money. 

1. If the dependent clause contains a compound tense, the finite verb, that is, the part of the verb that agrees with the subject, is placed at the end of the clause: 

Sage mir, was du alles gekauft hast. Tell me all that you have bought. a. In a dependent clause containing a present perfect or past perfect tense the auxiliary haben or ſein may be omitted, leaving the participle at the end of the clause: 

Jenes Buch dort ist Heines „Buch der Lieder," welches ich mit

Onkel Wilhelms Geld gekauft (habe). That book (yonder) is Heine's "Book of Songs," which I (have) bought with 

Uncle William's money. 

29. Word Order: Position of Objects and Adverbs 

1. Of two noun objects, a dative and an accusative, the dative stands first:

Der Vater gab seinem Sohne zwanzig Mark. The father gave his son twenty marks or The father gave twenty marks to his son. 

2. A pronominal object, whether dative or accusative, will usually precede a noun object:

Der Onkel gab mir dieses Geld. My uncle gave me this money. 

Die Mutter gab es ihrem Sohne. The mother gave it to her son.

[29 

3. Very frequently, in inverted or transposed order, a pronoun object is placed before a noun subject: 

Gestern führte mich der Freund meiner Mutter durch seine 

Wälder. Yesterday my mother's friend led me through his forests. 

Hier habe ich das Geld, welches mir der Onkel gegeben hat. Here 

I have the money that my uncle gave me. 

4. Of two pronoun objects the one bearing less stress will stand first: 

Er gab es mir gestern. He gave it to me yesterday.

Er gab mir alles, was er hatte. He gave me everything that he had. 

5. Objects, whether noun or pronoun, usually precede adverbs in all types of sentence structure, except in inverted order, where an adverb may be placed first for 

Ich las diese Gedichte geſtern in der Schule. 

Gestern hat uns der Lehrer diese Gedichte in der Schule erklärt. 

Ich war nicht in der Klasse, als der Lehrer das Gedicht gestern in der Schule erklärte. 

a. Exceptions to the general rules given above are matters of style rather than of syntax.

LESSON VI Verbs with Separable Prefixes 

1. From almost any verb the German may form many new ones by means of prefixes. Such prefixes are, for the most part, adverbs; but prepositions are frequently used, and also adjectives, nouns, and even infinitive forms of other verbs. Under certain conditions the prefixes are separated from the verb, and are therefore called separable prefixes, to distinguish them from the inseparable prefixes, discussed in article 17. 

2. Position of the separable prefix. In normal and inverted word order, with present, past, or imperative forms of the verb, the separable prefix is placed at the end of its clause; if the infinitive is used with zu this zu stands between the prefix and the verb; in the past participle ge stands between the prefix and the verb. The prefix and the verb are not separated when in the infinitive without zu, and when in present or past tense forms in transposed order. 

3. The following are examples of the various positions taken by the separable prefix: a. Principal parts: auf'stehen, stand auf, iſt aufgestanden (er steht auf) get up. b. Normal order: Ich stand von meinem Stuhle auf. I got up from my chair. c. Inverted order: Dann stand ich von meinem Stuhle auf. 

Then I got up from my chair. d. Infinitive with zu: Er bat mich, von meinem Stuhle aufzuſtehen. He asked me to get up from my chair. e. Past participle: Ich war von meinem Stuhle aufgestanden. 

I had got up from my chair. 51

[31, 32 

f. Imperative: Bitte, ſtehen Sie von Ihrem Stuhle auf. Please get up from your chair. g. Infinitive without zu: Ich kann nicht von meinem Stuhle aufstehen. I cannot get up from my chair. h. Transposed order: Mein Bruder kam ins Zimmer, als ich von meinem Stuhle aufstand. My brother came into the room as I got up from my chair. 31.

Position of Predicate Adjectives 

A predicate adjective has the same position as a separable prefix, but it is not regarded as an integral part of the verb and is not combined with it: 32.

Der Weg war lang gewesen. The way had been long. 

Ich sagte, daß die Abende jetzt sehr lang sind. I said that the evenings are now very long.

Die Abende fangen jetzt an, ſehr lang zu werden. The evenings are now beginning to become very long. 

Order of Clauses 

1. In a complex sentence, that is, a sentence containing a main clause and one or more dependent clauses, each clause is completed before the next is begun : 

Ich sagte, daß ich nicht zu Hause war, als der Mann uns besuchte. 

I said that I was not at home when the man visited us or I said that when the man visited us I was not at home. 

2. The German construes a dependent infinitive with its modifiers, which precede the infinitive, as a clause; such constructions are therefore subject to the rule stated above: 

In diesem Augenblick kam auch ein Schuhmann herbei, blieb bei den Autos stehen und fing an, die Leute auszufragen. At this moment a policeman came up also, stopped near the automobiles, and began to question the people.

33] 

LESSON VI 

3. The one exception to the general rule given above under 1 is that a relative clause may stand immediately after the noun that it modifies:

Ich sagte, daß der Schutzmann, welcher jetzt herbeikam, ansing, die Leute auszufragen. I said that the policeman, who now came up, began to question the people. 

4. In a complex sentence a dependent object clause or adverbial clause will frequently stand first; when this occurs, the main clause will have inverted order in accordance with the general rule given in 16, 3, a: 33. a. Object clause first: Daß der Vater nicht zu Hauſe iſt, habe ich dir schon gesagt. I have already told you that Father is not at home. b. Adverbial clause first: Als der Schutzmann herbeikam, fing er an, die Leute auszufragen. When the policeman came up, he began to question the people. 

Prepositions 

The genitive, dative, and accusative cases (oblique cases) are frequently dependent on prepositions. In the vocabularies of this text the case or cases governed by a preposition are indicated in italics. For the time being, further guidance is needed only when a preposition may govern either the dative or the accusative case. 

The following prepositions govern either the dative or the accusative, dependent on the construction in which they occur: an at, by, near, on, to auf on, upon hin'ter behind, after in in, into ne'ben beside, besides, next to, at the side of, by ü'ber over, across, above; concerning un'ter under, beneath, among vor before, in front of zwischen between

[33 

a. Prepositions and the definite article are frequently contracted: an dem to am an das to ans auf das to aufs hinter dem to hinterm in dem to im in das to ins über dem to überm über das to übers unter dem to unterm vor dem to vorm vor das to vors 

1. These prepositions govern the dative when used in expressions indicating rest, motion within restricted limits, or time when an event occurs: a. Rest: Paul saß in dem Garten unter einem Baume. Paul was sitting under a tree in the garden. b. Motion within restricted limits: Marie ging auf der Straße zwischen mir und meinem Bruder. Mary was walking in the street between me and my brother. c. Time: An jenem Tage blieben wir alle zu Hause. On that day we all stayed at home. 

2. These prepositions govern the accusative when used in expressions indicating motion to a place: 

Karl ging an das Fenster. Charles went to the window.

Der Mann flog über die Stadt. The man flew over (across) the city. 

3. Prepositions are frequently used figuratively in both 

English and German. It is usually possible for the student to see in the figurative use of a preposition the idea either of rest or of direction that will account for the case governed. 

Ich ging zu meinem Freunde in der Hoffnung, Hilfe bei ihm zu finden. I went to my friend in the hope of finding help with him (that is, resting in the hope of finding help). 

Ich denke an meinen Bruder. I am thinking of my brother (that is, my thoughts are directed toward my brother).

LESSON VII Strong Verbs: Formation of the Present Indicative 

Strong and weak verbs are inflected alike in the present indicative (article 4), except a few strong verbs having the stem vowel e long, those having the stem vowel e short, a, or au, and one with the vowel o. 

1. Of strong verbs having the stem vowel e long the following are of most frequent occurrence: le'ſen read befeh'len command ge'ben give ge'hen go gesche'hen happen he'ben lift neh'men take ſe'hen see ste'hen stand tre'ten step steh'len steal a. Gehen, stehen, and heben are inflected in the present indicative like weak verbs. b. Befehlen, geschehen, lesen, sehen, and stehlen change the stem vowel e to ie in the second and third person singular of the present indicative; in other forms of the present indicative the stem vowel is unchanged: ich sehe du siehst er sieht wir sehen ihr seht sie sehen 

Sie sehen c. Geben, nehmen, and treten change the e of the stem to i in the second and third person singular of the present indicative; in other forms of the present indicative the stem vowel is unchanged. Nehmen and treten show also certain consonantal irregularities. The complete inflection of the present indicative follows:

2. All strong verbs with the stem vowel e short change the e to i short in the second and third person singular of the present indicative: spre'chen speak ich spreche wir sprechen du sprichst er spricht ihr sprecht sie sprechen

LESSON VIII Declension of Adjectives 

When adjectives are used attributively, that is, immediately before the nouns they modify, they are declined according to one of the two models given below: 

STRONG DECLENSION, WITH DEFINITE CASE ENDINGS, LIKE dieſer 

The endings of the strong declension are used 

1. When the attributive adjective is not preceded by any pronominal or possessive adjective whatsoever, as shown in the model. 

2. When the attributive adjective is preceded by any uninflected form of a pronominal or possessive adjective. a. The ein-words are uninflected in three forms, the nominative singular of the masculine and neuter, and the accusative singular of the neuter; manch-, solch-, welch, and some other pronominal adjectives are frequently uninflected (27, 2, c, d, e); etwas, ein paar, and nichts nothing are uninflected. 

SINGULAR MASCULINE FEMININE NEUTER 

NOм. guter Mann gute Frau gutes Kind

GEN. 

guten Mannes 1 guter Frau guten Kindes ¹ 

DAT. gutem Manne guter Frau gutem Kinde

Acc. guten Mann gute Frau gutes Kind

PLURAL 

NOм. gute Männer gute Frauen gute Kinder GEN. guter Männer guter Frauen guter Kinder 

DAT. guten Männern guten Frauen guten Kindern Acc. gute Männer gute Kinder 

¹ See page 64, "Exceptions and Further Details," 1. gute Frauen 63

[36 

 WEAK DECLENSION, WITH INDEFINITE CASE ENDINGS 

The endings of the weak declension are used 1. When the attributive adjective is preceded by der, dieser, or any pronominal adjective declined like dieser (27, 1, 2). 

2. When the attributive adjective is preceded by an inflected form of any of the ein-words (14, 1, 2). 

NOм. der gute Mann 

GEN. des guten Mannes 

DAT. dem guten Manne 

Acc. den guten Mann

SINGULAR 

die gute Frau der guten Frau der guten Frau die gute Frau

PLURAL das gute Kind des guten Kindes dem guten Kinde das gute Kind

Acc. NOM. die guten Männer 

GEN. der guten Männer 

DAT. den guten Männern die guten Männer die guten Frauen der guten Frauen die guten Kinder der guten Kinder den guten Frauen den guten Kindern die guten Frauen die guten Kinder

SUMMARY OF THE ADJECTIVE DECLENSION 

1. Attributive adjectives not preceded by endings take strong endings, those of dieſer. 

2. Attributive adjectives preceded by endings take weak endings: e in five cases (nominative singular of the masculine, feminine, and neuter, and accusative singular of the feminine and neuter), -en in all other cases. 

EXCEPTIONS AND FURTHER DETAILS 

1. Before masculine and neuter nouns ending in the genitive singular in -8 or -es the attributive adjective adds -en instead of -es in the genitive singular : ein Glas kalten Waſſers a glass of cold water. (In such cases the noun has definite endings; the adjective, therefore, has the indefinite ending of the weak declension.)

36] 

LESSON VIII 

2. The adjective regularly has the strong ending -e in the nominative and accusative plural after andere, einige, mehrere, viele, wenige, and occasionally after alle, beide, manche, solche: viele schöne Bücher many beautiful books 3. Adjectives ending in unaccented -el, en, -er, usually drop the e of this syllable before an -e of the inflectional ending: dunkel dark eine dunkle Nacht a dark night 

4. Two attributive adjectives before a noun will have the same endings: guter, alter Mann dem guten, alten Manne 

5. Predicate adjectives are not inflected:

Die Tage sind jetzt sehr lang. 

6. The student will occasionally meet adjectives in settings not explained by the rules that have preceded; he should note such constructions when he meets them. 

7. Adjectives used as nouns retain their adjective inflection, but are written with an initial capital letter: der Alte the old man etwas Gutes something good a. In the German equivalent of such phrases as a good one, the old one, and the like, the adjective is not regarded as a noun; it is therefore written with a small initial letter: 

Marie kaufte ein neues Kleid und gab ihrer Schweſter ihr altes. 

Mary bought a new dress and gave her old one to her sister. 

8. Of the pronominal adjectives discussed in article 27, 2, 3, ander-, beid-, viel-, wenig-, may be preceded by der or words inflected like dieſer or ein; jed- and solch are frequently preceded by ein; ein is frequently preceded by the definite article. In such cases the second word of the

group is regarded as an adjective and follows the rules of the adjective declension: 

ein andrer Mann ein jeder each one die beiden (two) Freunde eine solche such a (one) unsere vielen (wenigen) Freunde our many (few) friends 9. In its uninflected form, almost any adjective may be used as an adverb: alt old arm poor ein schnelles Auto a fast automobile 

Er fährt schnell He drives fast

LESSON IX Reflexive Verbs 

1. Reflexive verbs, that is, verbs having as their object the same person as the subject, are of far more frequent occurrence in German than in English. Very frequently they are the equivalent of English intransitive verbs or 

2. sich är'gern be vexed sich freu'en be glad, be happy sich erfäl'ten catch cold fich verbeu'gen bow 

PRESENT INDICATIVE OF A REFLEXIVE VERB 

sich set'zen sit down, seat oneself ich setze mich wir setzen uns du setz (es)t dich ihr setzt euch er, sie, es setzt sich sie setzen sich 

Sie setzen sich 3. The various tense forms of reflexive verbs are in no way different from those of other verbs: 

Past: ich setzte mich etc. 

Present perfect: ich habe mich gesetzt etc. 

Past perfect: ich hatte mich geſetzt etc. a. Much less frequent than reflexive verbs with an accusative object are those with a dative or a genitive object; the dative objects appear as mir, dir, sich, uns, euch, sich; the genitive objects appear as meiner, deiner, ſeiner, ihrer, seiner, unser, euer, ihrer, 

Ich schmeichle mir. I flatter myself.

Er schonte seiner selbst nicht. He did not spare himself. b. Reflexive and personal pronouns are identical except in the dative and accusative cases of the third person. 69

[38, 39 

38. 

Impersonal Verbs 

1. Impersonal verbs are used very frequently in both 

German and English to refer to natural phenomena: 

Es reg'net. It is raining. 

Es don'nerte. It was thundering.

Es ist heiß gewesen. It was hot. 

Es war kalt geworden. It had become cold. 

2. Impersonal verbs are used in German more frequently than in English to express feeling or emotion: 

Es tut mir leid. I am sorry. 

Es är'gerte mich. I was vexed, It vexed me. 

Es freut mich. I am glad, It makes me glad. a. An impersonal verb with pronoun object will appear in the following forms, depending on whether a dative or accusative case is used after the verb: 

Es tut mir leid. 

Es tut dir leid. 

Es tut ihm leid. 

Es freut mich. 

Es freut dich. 

Es freut ihn. 

Es tut ihr (her) leid. Etc. 

Es freut fie (her). Etc. b. It is evident from a comparison of examples under 37, 1, and 38, 2, that the German sometimes expresses essentially the same thought in two different ways: 

I am vexed. Ich ärgere mich or Es ärgert mich. 

He is glad. Er freut sich or Es freut ihn.

In such cases it makes no difference which of the two idioms is used.

LESSON X 

Wer'den become

Werden is a strong verb with irregular forms in both the present and the past tense; the principal parts are werden, ward or wurde, ist geworden. Of the two past forms ward can be used only in the singular.

PRESENT INDICATIVE PAST INDICATIVE 

wir wurden ich werde wir werden du wirst ihr werdet ich ward or wurde du wardſt or wurdest ihr wurdet er wird sie werden er ward or wurde ſie wurden 

Sie werden 

Sie wurden 41. 

Formation of the Future Tense 

1. The future tense of a verb is formed by adding its infinitive to the present forms of werden: wir werden sehen we shall see ich werde sehen I shall see du wirst ſehen you will see ihr werdet sehen you will see er wird ſehen he will see sie werden sehen they will see 

Sie werden sehen you will see a. The difficulties caused by the difference between the declarative and the interrogative forms of the future in English do not exist in German, as will be seen by comparing the following paradigm with the one above. shall I see? werde ich sehen? shall you see? wirſt du ſehen? shall we see? werden wir sehen? shall you see? werdet ihr sehen? will they see? werden sie sehen? will he see? wird er sehen? shall you see? werden Sie sehen? b. A complementary infinitive, like a dependent infinitive, is preceded by its modifiers:

Wir werden das Gedicht von Goethe heute in der Schule lesen. 74

LESSON XI Relative Pronouns The German uses the following relative pronouns: 

der who, that, which wel'cher who, that, which mēr he who, whoever was that, that which, whatever 1. The inflection of der as a relative pronoun is given below; the forms in which the use of der as a relative differs from those of the definite article are printed in heavy-faced type: 

SINGULAR PLURAL MASCULINE FEMININE NEUTER ALL GENDERS 

NOM. der die das die 

GEN. dessen deren deſſen deren 

DAT. Dem der dem denen Acc. den die das die 

2. Welcher is inflected like dieſer (27, 1). 

3. The German uses der or welcher as a relative indiscriminately, except in the genitive cases, where the forms of der are obligatory:

Das ist der Herr, mit dessen Schwester Sie soeben getanzt haben. 

That is the gentleman with whose sister you have just danced. 

4. Wer and was are inflected only in the singular. NOM. wer GEN. weſſen

DAT. wem Acc. wen was weſſen (lacking) was 78

44] 

LESSON XI 

5. For reference to a definite person or object as antecedent only der or welcher may be used as a relative pronoun, never wer or was:

Dies ist der Freund, von dem or von welchem ich gestern gesprochen habe. 

6. Was is used as relative pronoun when referring to indefinite neuter pronouns like alles, etwas, nichts, and to neuter forms of adjectives used as nouns :

Ich gab dem armen Manne alles, was ich bei mir hatte. I gave the poor man everything that I had with me. 

Das Schönste, was ich gesehen habe. The most beautiful thing that I saw.

Was may not be used to refer to a definite object as antecedent. 

44. The Demonstrative Pronoun der that, that one, he, she, it 1. Der when used as a demonstrative pronoun is inflected like der the relative (43, 1). The distinction between the relative and the demonstrative is immediately clear to the German, since the relative is always followed by transposed order. a. The genitive of the demonstrative der is little used, but it serves a useful purpose as a substitute for a possessive adjective where the antecedent of the possessive might be ambiguous: 

Dies sind Freundinnen von Marie Breithaupt und sind mit deren 

Brüdern gestern von Dresden mit einem Flugzeug herübergeflogen. These are friends of Mary Breithaupt, and (they) flew over from Dresden yesterday with brothers of the latter in a plane.

If the phrase ihren Brüdern had been used, the listener would have been in doubt whose brothers were meant, Mary's or her friends'.

[45, 46 

b. Derer, an infrequent genitive plural form of the demonstrative der, occurs: die Kinder derer, die warteten the children of those who were waiting. 

45. The Interrogative Pronouns welcher, wer, was 

1. When used as an interrogative pronoun welcher is inflected like dieser (27, 1). Wer and was, when used as interrogatives, have the same inflection as the relatives wer and was (43, 4). 

2. Was is uninflected in the phrases was für ein (sing.) and was für (pl.) what kind of; ein is not affected by für, but agrees in gender, number, and case with the noun that follows: 

Was für eine Feder ist das? What kind of pen is that?

Mit was für einer Feder hat er den Brief geschrieben? With what kind of pen did he write the letter? 

Was für Federn sind das? What kinds of pens are those? 

46. The Neuter Singular Pronouns es, dies, das, with ſein 1. Before the verb ſein, when the purpose of the speaker is to identify or characterize more fully persons or objects that have already been referred to, German expresses he, she, it, and they by es this and these by dies that and those by das

Forms of the verb ſein agree in number with the predicate noun or pronoun that follows. The question Wer ist der junge Herr? is answered, Es ist mein Freund; the question Wer sind die jungen Damen? is answered, Es sind

47, 48] 81 

LESSON XI 

die Töchter von Frau books; those are my old ones, one says, Dies sind meine neuen Bücher, das sind meine alten. 

To express These are my new 2. In a similar fashion es is used as predicate after singular or plural forms of ſein following personal or demonstrative pronouns in the singular or plural as subject: 47. 

Ich bin es. It is I. 

Sie sind es. It is you. 

Ist er es? Is it he? 

Sind fie es? Is it they?

Die war es. It was she, She was the one. 

Die waren es. It was they, They were the ones. 

Das ist es. That is it, It is that. 

Polite Forms 

1. When mentioning one member of a family to another in German it is customary, except among intimate friends, to say, Ihr Herr Vater, Ihre Frau Mutter, Ihr Herr Sohn, Ihre Fräulein Tochter, Ihr Herr Bruder, Ihre Fräulein 

Schwester, rather than Jhr Vater, Jhre Mutter, and so on. a. Genitive forms corresponding to the above are Ihres Herrn 

Vaters, Ihrer Frau Mutter, Ihrer Fräulein Schwester, and so on.

The tendency is to construe Fräulein Schwester as a unit, feminine in gender, with which the possessive adjective Ihr- agrees. 48. 

Proper Nouns after Generic Terms 

After words indicating a class, a group, an institution, or the like, the German construes the name of the specific member of the group in apposition: die Familie Breithaupt the Breithaupt family die Stadt Leipzig the city of Leipzig die Universität München the University of Munich

LESSON XII Modal Auxiliaries 

Both English and German have a group of verbs called modal auxiliaries; they are characterized by irregularity of inflection and a considerable variety of meaning. Because these verbs lack many of the usual inflectional forms in English, for which a variety of circumlocutions are used, the English-speaking student finds the use of the German modal auxiliaries especially difficult. These auxiliaries are dür'fen may, be permitted to müſ'ſen must, be compelled to, have to fön'nen can, be able to fol'len shall, ought to; be to; be said to, be supposed to mö'gen like, care to; be pos- wollen will, want to, intend to, be sible about to; claim to 

1. Three of the modal auxiliaries are taken up in this lesson. They are, with their principal parts, dürfen, durfte, gedurft may, be permitted to können, konnte, gefonnt can, be able to sollen, sollte, gesollt shall, ought to, be to, be said to, be supposed to 2.

Sie durften

1. Dürfen expresses permission. 

ich darf I may, I am permitted to, I am allowed to ich darf nicht I must not; (less frequently) I am not at liberty to, I am not free to, I am not permitted to, I cannot 

2. Können expresses ability when used with a personal subject, possibility when used with an impersonal subject. ich kann I can, I am able es kann (ſein) it may (be), it may possibly (be); possibly 

3. Sollen expresses moral obligation, is used in statements concerning one's duty, or expresses a supposition concerning a fact or condition. er ſoll he shall, he is to; he is said to, he is supposed to a. Sollen is never used as auxiliary of the future tense. 51.

Nouns of Class V 

A limited number of masculine and neuter nouns are inflected like nouns of Class I, II, or III in the singular, and like nouns of Class IV in the plural. Because of this difference of inflection in the singular and plural, nouns of Class V are frequently said to have mixed declension, or to belong to the Mixed Declension.

Herzens, dem Herzen, das Herz; Herzen in all cases of the plural. 

2. Of the nouns of Class V the only ones that can be definitely assigned to this group by their form are those ending in -or. For the identification of nouns of this class the genitive singular and the nominative plural are usually given in elementary vocabularies. a. Nouns ending in the nominative singular in or are accented on the preceding syllable in the singular, and on the o in the plural, as shown in the model. b. Nouns like Name often appear with a nominative singular form in -n, Namen; they are then regular nouns of Class I. c. A small number of nouns of foreign origin form the genitive singular and the entire plural, including the dative, with -8: das Auto, des Autos, die Autos der Bankier banker, des Bankiers, die Bankiers das Bureau office, des Bureaus, die Bureaus 

d. Colloquially some native German words form the plural (including the dative) with 8: das Mädel girl, die Mädels der Junge boy, die Jungens (a double plural) The foreigner will do well to follow more conventional usage.

[52, 53 

52. Der'jenige he, the one; derselbe the same 

Derjenige is used only as a pronoun and is usually followed by a relative pronoun; derselbe is used both as a pronoun and as an adjective. Each of the component parts of these words is inflected throughout — der like the definite article, the second portion as if an adjective of the form jenig, ſelb-. The component parts are not separated: masculine, derselbe, desselben, demſelben, denselben, etc. 53. 

Polite Forms 

1. In formal society an unmarried woman is frequently addressed as Gnädiges Fräulein, rather than by her name, Fräulein; a married woman, as Gnädige Frau. The 

German addresses a gentleman as Herr Doktor, Herr Oberſt, when in English we should say, Doctor Colonel A married woman receives her husband's title when she is addressed or spoken of:

Frau Doktor 

Frau Professor

Frau Doktor Keller die Frau Doktor 2. When proper names are preceded by words of address or title, the forms of the oblique cases, especially those of the genitive, are a source of uncertainty even to a German at times. When the word Herr occurs in connection with titles or proper nouns it is always inflected in the oblique cases. The following forms of expression have the approval of German authorities: a. In the genitive, if the title is preceded by the definite article, decline the definite article and the title (except feminine nouns), but not the proper noun:

Dies ist das Haus des Herrn (der Frau, des Fräuleins) Keller. 

Dies ist das Haus des Doktors Keller. 

Dies ist das Haus des Herrn Doktors Keller.

53] 

LESSON XII 

b. If the title is not preceded by the definite article, decline the proper noun, but not the title (except Herr):

Dies ist Herrn Kellers (Frau Kellers, Fräulein Kellers) Haus. 

Dies ist Doktor Kellers Haus. 

Dies ist Herrn Doktor Kellers Haus.

PRESENT INDICATIVE

Mögen, müſſen, and wollen defined as used in the Indicative Mood 

1. Mögen has two very distinct meanings: (1) like or (with negatives) dislike, (2) be possible or probable. 

Ich mag den Mann nicht. I do not like the man.

Ich mochte nicht gehen. I did not care to go, I did not want to go. 92

55] 

LESSON XIII 

Er mag frank sein.

He may (possibly) be sick, Perhaps he is sick. 

Er mochte eine Stunde gegangen sein. He had been walking for perhaps an hour. a. Both mögen and fönnen, it will be observed, are sometimes used to express possibility. b. Mögen seems occasionally to have much the same meaning as dürfen, but the implication is of a permission granted grudgingly:

Nun ja, er mag gehen, wenn er will. Very well then, he may go (let him go) if he wants to. 

2. Müſſen expresses necessity, compulsion. be- Ich mußte zu Hause bleiben, weil ich krank war- weil der Vater es wollte. I had to stay at home, because I was sick cause Father wanted me to.

So eine Reise muß intereſſant ſein. Such a journey must be interesting (that is, cannot but be interesting). 

3. Wollen expresses willingness, determination, insistence, a claim to, the first step in the execution of one's intention.

Ich will zu Hause bleiben. I will stay at home, I intend to stay at home.

Ich wollte eben nach Hause gehen. I was just going to (was just about to) go home.

Der Mann will das gesehen haben. The man claims to have seen that. a. The idea of insistence contained in wollen is frequently transferred to an inanimate object:

Der Regen wollte nicht aufhören. The rain would not stop, It seemed as if the rain would never stop. b. Wollen is never used as auxiliary of the future tense.

[56, 57 

56. 

Omission of Complementary Infinitives 

After the modal auxiliaries the German frequently omits a complementary infinitive required by English idiom. The construction occurs in two distinct types: 

1. Complementary infinitives expressing motion are usually omitted, and the direction of the motion indicated by an adverb or an adverbial phrase:

Ich muß jetzt nach Hause. I must go home now. 

2. A complementary infinitive is frequently omitted, and its place taken by es or das; to such a question as 

Darf ich das Buch lesen? the answer is Ja, du darfst es or 

Ja, das darfst du, Yes, you may or Yes, you may read it. 

57. Es as Expletive and as Grammatical Subject 

1. The neuter singular pronoun es is frequently used before ſein in German as is the English expletive there before the verb be; in number the verb agrees with the predicate that follows:

Es war nur ein Buch auf dem Tische. There was only one book on the table.

Es waren viele Bücher auf dem Tische. There were many books on the table. 

2. Somewhat similar to the usage just described, but frequently without equivalent in English, is the use of es before almost any verb with a following noun (sometimes a pronoun) subject. Es is called the grammatical subject, and the noun or pronoun that follows is called the logical subject; the verb agrees in number with the logical subject:

Es trat ein Mann aus dem Haus. A man stepped out of the house.

58] 

LESSON XIII 

Es traten drei Männer aus dem Haus. Three men stepped out of the house. 

Es antwortete ihm niemand. No one answered him. 

Es antworteten ihm alle. They all answered him. 58. 

Use or Omission of ein

Before words indicating a profession or trade the German uses the indefinite article only when the word is preceded by a descriptive adjective: 

Mein Freund ist Arzt. My friend is a physician.

Mein Freund ist ein berühmter Arzt. My friend is a famous physician.

LESSON XIV 

Irregular Weak Verbs The following weak verbs show a change of vowel in the past and the past participle and are, for this reason, called irregular weak verbs. Two of them, bringen and denken, like their cognates in English, show a change of consonant in the past and past participle: brin'gen, brachte, gebracht bring, take, carry bren'nen, brannte, gebrannt burn den'ken, dachte, gedacht think fen'nen, fannte, gefannt know, be acquainted with nen'nen, nannte, genannt name, call by name sen'den, sandte, gesandt send wen'den, wandte, gewandt turn 1. The inflection of the irregular weak verbs, once their principal parts are known, offers no difficulty whatsoever, since the various person and tense forms are developed from the principal parts in strictly regular fashion. For example,

While these forms offer no difficulty, the student will do better to avoid their use. b. Distinguish between fennen, which means know in the sense of be acquainted with, and wiſſen, which means know facts, have information. 99 66

LESSON XV Past Subjunctive Forms 

1. The personal endings added to the past stem in forming the past subjunctive are the same for all verbs,weak, strong, modal auxiliaries, irregular weak verbs, and the auxiliaries haben, sein, and werden. They are =e -en =et =eſt =e =en 2. The past subjunctive forms of regular weak verbs and of the modal auxiliaries sollen and wollen are identical with the indicative forms: 

ich fragte wir fragten du fragtest ihr fragtet er fragte fie fragten ich sollte wir sollten du solltest ihr solltet er ſollte ſie ſollten 

Sie sollten

Sie fragten 3. Strong verbs, including fein, with the vowel a, o, or u in the past stem umlaut the stem vowel and add the personal endings: ſein fahren ich wäre wir wären ich führe wir führen du wärest ihr wäret du führest ihr führet er wäre ſie wären er führe sie führen Sie wären 

Sie führen

Strong verbs with other vowels than a, o, or u in the past stem add only the personal endings. a. The student will occasionally encounter ö or it where he would expect ä in the past subjunctive of strong verbs. In the list of strong verbs beginning on page 185 this irregularity is given in the last column. 103

[60, 61, 62 

4. Past subjunctive forms of haben, werden, wissen, the modal auxiliaries (except ſollen and wollen), and the irregular weak verbs bringen and denken are like the past indicative, except that the stem vowel is umlauted: ich hätte ich würde ich wüßte etc. ich dürfte ich könnte ich brächte ich dächte ich möchte ich müßte etc. etc. a. The past subjunctive forms of werden are made only from the past indicative wurde (not from ward). 

5. Preterit subjunctive forms of the irregular weak verbs brennen, kennen, nennen, rennen, change the past stem vowel to e and add the personal endings: ich brennte ich kennte ich nennte ich rennte etc. 

Senden and wenden have the past subjunctive forms ich sendete, ich wendete, etc. 

61. Formation of the Past Perfect Subjunctive 

The past perfect subjunctive forms of a verb are made by adding its past participle to the past subjunctive forms of haben or sein, in accordance with the rules given in article 21, 3, a and b. ich hätte gesehen ich wäre gegangen du hättest gesehen du wärest gegangen er hätte gesehen er wäre gegangen etc. etc. 62. 

Forms of the Conditional Mood 

The present conditional and the perfect conditional forms of a verb are made by adding, respectively, the infinitive and the perfect infinitive of the verb to the past subjunctive forms of werden.

63, 64] 105 

LESSON XV 

1. 

MODEL OF THE PRESENT CONDITIONAL 

ich würde gehen I should go du würdest gehen you would go er würde gehen he would go

I go? should you go? would he go? and similarly in the plural. 2. 

MODELS OF THE PERFECT CONDITIONAL 

ich würde geschrieben haben du würdest geschrieben haben er würde geschrieben haben wir würden geschrieben haben ihr würdet geschrieben haben sie würden geschrieben haben 

Sie würden geschrieben haben 

I should have written etc. 63. ich würde gegangen sein du würdest gegangen sein er würde gegangen sein wir würden gegangen sein ihr würdet gegangen sein sie würden gegangen ſein 

Sie würden gegangen sein 

I should have gone etc.

Use of the Subjunctive Mood 

The subjunctive mood is used to express possibility, contingency, concession, wish, desire, purpose, or uncertainty. 64. 

Conditional Sentences 

Because of the meanings that attach to the subjunctive mood its use is especially frequent in conditional sentences, a type of sentence in which it is assumed that a certain thing might be, might take place, or might have taken place, contingent upon the fulfillment of a certain condition.

The past subjunctive is used in both clauses of a conditional sentence to represent the supposed case as possible, though uncertain, in future time, or as contrary to fact in present time.

[65 

Possibility in future time: Es wäre sehr schön, wenn der Bruder morgen nach Hause käme. It would be very pleasant 

Brother came home (should come home) tomorrow.

Contrary to fact in present time: Der Tag wäre sehr schön, wenn der Wind nicht so kalt wäre. The day would be very beautiful if the wind were not so cold.

The past perfect subjunctive is used in both clauses to represent the supposed case as contrary to fact in past time:

Contrary to fact in past time: Er hätte uns gestern besucht, wenn er nicht frank gewesen wäre. He would have visited us yesterday if he had not been sick. a. There is a type of conditional sentence in which the time referred to is not clearly present time and not clearly future; in such a sentence the condition is said to be a less vivid future condition :

Wir könnten unsere Freunde auf dem Lande besuchen, wenn der Tag schön wäre. We might visit our friends in the country if the day were beautiful or if the day should be beautiful.

This type of conditional sentence presents no additional difficulty, because the same subjunctive forms are used in German whether the time referred to is present or future, whether the supposed case may possibly be realized in future time or is contrary to fact in present time. b. When the indicative mood is used in the if-clause, in the conclusion, or in both clauses of a conditional sentence, it indicates that the speaker or writer feels certain that the supposed case or the conclusion or both are in accordance with fact. 65. 

Various Forms of Conditional Sentences 

1. In the previous illustrations of conditional sentences the main clause was in all cases placed first and was followed by the subordinate clause. This arrangement is in

65] 

LESSON XV 

no way necessary. Quite as often as not the subordinate clause is expressed first; the main clause then has inverted order and is usually introduced by the adverb ſo: 

Wenn der Tag schön wäre, so könnten wir unſere Freunde auf dem Lande besuchen. 

2. Very frequently in German, as in English, the condition is expressed by means of inverted order rather than by the word if:

Hätte ich das gewußt, so wäre ich nicht gekommen. Had I known that, I should not have come. 

3. In the main clause of a conditional sentence the German frequently uses the forms of the conditional mood instead of the past or past perfect forms of the subjunctive: 

Wenn es nicht so kalt wäre, so würde der Tag sehr schön sein. 

If it were not so cold, the day would be very beautiful. 

4. The forms of the conditional mood are never used in the if-clause of a conditional sentence. 

5. From what has just been said it is evident that the following combinations of clauses are possible in condi tional sentences: 

Es wäre sehr schön,

LESSON XVI Present Subjunctive Forms 

1. The personal endings of the present subjunctive are the same as those of the past subjunctive: singular, -e, -est, e; plural, en, et, en. The present subjunctive forms. of all verbs except ſein are made by adding the personal endings to the present stem, which is found by dropping the -en (or n) of the infinitive. The present subjunctive of ſein shows certain irregularities. 2.
