68. The Subjunctive in Indirect Discourse 

Because the subjunctive mood is used to indicate uncertainty on the part of the speaker, it is regularly used in indirect quotations when the statement is attributed to another person and is not vouched for by the one speaking. 

1. Change of mood in indirect discourse. The rule govern ing the use of mood and tense in indirect discourse, stated in its strictest form, is as follows: In indirect quotations and in indirect questions, use the same tense that was used in the original direct statement or direct question, but substitute the subjunctive mood for the indicative: 

Direct statement: Karl sagt (or sagte): „Ich sehe den Mann auf der Straße.“

Indirect statement: Karl ſagt (or ſagte), daß er den Mann auf der Straße sehe. 

Direct statement: Karl sagt (or sagte): „Ich habe den Mann auf der Straße gesehen.“

Indirect statement: Karl sagt (or sagte), daß er den Mann auf der Straße gesehen habe. 

Direct statement: Karl sagt (or sagte): „Ich werde morgen nicht zu Hause sein."

Indirect statement: Karl sagt (or sagte), daß er morgen nicht zu Hause sein werde. a. The English method of indicating indirect discourse for the above sentences is: 

Charles says that he sees (said that he saw).

Charles says that he has seen (said that he had seen). 

Charles says that he shall not be (said that he should not be).

68] 

LESSON XVI 

2. Change of both mood and tense in indirect discourse.

When indicative and subjunctive forms of the present tense are identical, as is usually the case in the first person singular, the first person plural, and the third person plural, use in the indirect discourse a past subjunctive form for the present indicative form of the direct discourse: 

Direct statement: Karl und Paul sagen (or sagten): „Wir sehen den Mann auf der Straße."

Indirect statement: Karl und Paul sagen (or ſagten), daß ſie den Mann auf der Straße sähen. 

Direct statement: Karl und Paul sagen (or sagten): „Wir haben den Mann auf der Straße gesehen." 

Indirect statement: Karl und Paul sagen (or sagten), daß sie den Mann auf der Straße gesehen hätten. 

Direct statement: Karl und Paul sagen (or sagten): „Wir werden morgen nicht zu Hause sein."

Indirect statement: Karl und Paul sagen (or sagten), daß sie morgen nicht zu Hause sein würden. a. It is evident from the examples above that by changing the forms of the auxiliaries haben, sein, and werden from present to past subjunctive, the present perfect and future tenses are changed, respectively, to the past perfect subjunctive and the present conditional. Under similar circumstances the infrequent future perfect indicative takes on the form of the perfect conditional. 

3. Change of past indicative to present perfect or past perfect subjunctive. When the direct discourse has a past indicative form of the verb, the indirect discourse will have a present perfect subjunctive form or, under the conditions explained under 2 above, a past perfect subjunctive form: 

Direct statement: Karl sagt (or sagte): „Geſtern sah ich den 

Mann auf der Straße."

Indirect statement: Karl ſagt (or fagte), daß er den Mann gestern auf der Straße gesehen habe.

[68 

Direct statement: Karl und Paul sagen (or sagten): „Gestern sahen wir den Mann auf der Straße."

Indirect statement: Karl und Paul sagen (or sagten), daß sie den Mann gestern auf der Straße gesehen hätten. 

4. Summary of rules governing the use of mood and tense in indirect discourse. In indirect discourse use the same tense that was used in the direct discourse, but substitute the subjunctive mood for the indicative. 

EXCEPTION 1. When indicative and subjunctive forms are identical, substitute the past subjunctive for the present, the past perfect subjunctive for the present perfect, and the present conditional for the future.

EXCEPTION 2. For the past indicative of direct discourse substitute the present perfect subjunctive (or past perfect subjunctive). a. In both the spoken and the written language many Germans frequently use past, past perfect, or conditional forms where, according to the rules given above, present, present perfect, or future forms are demanded. More careful speakers and writers observe the rules closely. 

5. Either indicative or subjunctive in indirect discourse. 

Since the indicative mood is used to express reality or certainty, and the subjunctive mood to express possibility or uncertainty, there are some constructions in German in which either mood may be used, although in English one form of expression is used in both cases:

Ich habe gehört, daß Herr Schmidt gestorben ist. I have heard (and I know it is true) that Mr. Smith has died. 

Ich habe gehört, daß Herr Schmidt gestorben sei. I have heard (so the rumor goes) that Mr. Smith has died. 

Ich glaube, daß er ein guter Mann ist. I believe (am convinced) that he is a good man.

Ich glaube, daß er ein guter Mann sei. I believe (but am not certain) that he is a good man.

69, 70] 115 

LESSON XVI 

6. Subjunctive forms of direct discourse are taken over in the same mood and tense forms in indirect discourse: 

Direct statement: Karl sagt (or sagte): „Ich würde mit Ihnen gehen, wenn der Tag nicht so kalt wäre.“

Indirect statement: Karl sagt (or ſagte), daß er mit uns gehen würde, wenn der Tag nicht so kalt wäre. 

7. Indirect discourse may be expressed in German, as in English, without the use of the introductory subordi nating conjunction: 

Karl sagte, er habe den Mann auf der Straße gesehen. Charles said he had seen the man on the street. 

69. Indirect Quotation of Commands and Requests 

1. In the indirect quotation of a command after the verb fagen a circumlocution with ſollen is used:

Der Lehrer sagte, daß ich mit meinem Bruder nach Hause gehen folle. The teacher told me to go home with my brother. 

2. In the indirect quotation of a request a circumlocution with mögen is frequently used: 

Die Kinder bettelten den Vater an, er möge ihnen einen Drachen faufen. The children implored their father to buy them a kite. a. After befehlen command and usually after bitten ask the indirect quotation is expressed, as in English, by means of an infinitive construction : 70. 

Der Lehrer befahl mir (bat mich), mit dem Bruder nach Hause zu gehen. 

Als, wenn, wann

The English word when is expressed in German by three different words: 

1. Als is used to refer only to one definite past time: 

Als er letzte Woche in der Stadt war, besuchte er seine alten Freunde. 

When he was in town last week, he visited his old friends.

[71 

2. Wenn means when in the sense of whenever, and in this sense may refer to present, past, or future time:

Wenn er in der Stadt war, besuchte er seine alten Freunde. 

Whenever he was in the city, he visited his old friends. 

Wenn er in der Stadt ist, besucht er seine alten Freunde. Whenever he is in the city, he visits his old friends. 

3. Wann is used only in direct or indirect questions:

Wann wird Paul zu Hause sein, Frau Keller? When will Paul be at home, Mrs. Keller?

Ich fragte Frau Keller, wann Paul zu Hause sein werde. I asked Mrs. Keller when Paul would be at home. 71. 

Wenn, ob 1. Wenn in the sense of if is used only in conditional 

Wenn er morgen zu Hause ist, so werde ich ihn besuchen. If he is at home tomorrow, I shall call on him. 

2. Ob if is used only in indirect questions:

Ich fragte Frau Keller, ob Paul zu Hause sei. I asked 

Mrs. Keller if Paul was at home.

LESSON XVII Modal Auxiliaries SOME ENGLISH EQUIVALENTS OF THE PRESENT PERFECT AND PAST PERFECT TENSES 

When a modal auxiliary is used in the present perfect or the past perfect tense with an infinitive dependent upon it, the infinitive of the modal auxiliary is used instead of the past participle. When this construction occurs in transposed order, the auxiliary haben precedes the two infinitives:

Normal order: Ich habe heute nicht kommen können. I could not (have not been able to) come today.

Transposed order: Paul sagte, daß er heute nicht habe kommen fönnen. Paul said that he could not (had not been able to) come today.

Heinrich wäre schon da, wenn er hätte kommen dürfen. Henry would already be here, if he had been allowed to come. 

2. A few other verbs, most frequently helfen, hören, laſſen, ſehen, are subject to the same rules as those given above for the modal auxiliaries :

Vater sagte, daß er den Arzt habe kommen laſſen. Father said that he had sent for the doctor (had let the doctor come). a. In accordance with the general rule that an infinitive is preceded by its modifiers, the dependent infinitive regularly precedes the governing infinitive in the constructions described above. b. The practice of placing the tense auxiliary before the two dependent infinitives in transposed order has been extended to include future and conditional forms:

Die Kinder sagten, daß sie heute nicht würden kommen können. 

The children said that they should not be able to come today. 119

[73 

c. When the modal auxiliaries are used in the perfect tenses without a dependent infinitive, the past participle of the modal is used and unmodified transposed order: 

Warum ist Paul heute nicht mitgekommen?— Weil er es nicht gedurft hat. Why did Paul not come today? Because he wasn't allowed to. 

3. Because English modal auxiliaries are defective in the past participle, the English equivalents of German present perfect and past perfect tenses of the modals offer some difficulty to the English-speaking student. Note the 

73.

Paul wäre gekommen, wenn er hätte kommen dürfen (können, müſſen). Paul would have come if he had been allowed (been able, been made) to come.

Paul hätte kommen können (dürfen), wenn er hätte kommen mögen (wollen). Paul could have (might have) come if he had cared to (wanted to) come. 

Paul hat es tun sollen. Paul was to have done it. 

Paul hat es nicht getan, wenn er es auch hätte tun ſollen. Paul did not do it, even though he ought to have (or should have) done it.

Some English Equivalents of the Past 

Subjunctive of German Modals 

Dürfen: Ich dürfte das nicht tun. I should not be allowed to do that or I should not feel free to do that.

Das dürfte wohl geschehen. That might well happen. 

Können: Ich könnte das tun. I might do that, I could do that. 

Mögen: Ich möchte das tun. I should like to do that.

Sollen: Ich sollte das tun. I ought to do that. (The indicative, ich sollte das tun, is identical in form, but means I was to do that.)

It has been possible, in the brief presentation of English equivalents of German modal auxiliaries, to indicate only a limited number of their many idiomatic uses. Careful

74] 

LESSON XVII 

attention to the context in which the modals occur will enable the student to enlarge his knowledge of their many meanings. 74.

Prefixes Separable or Inseparable 

Besides the many separable prefixes and the seven inseparable prefixes used to form verbal compounds, there is a smaller group of prefixes used to form compounds that are sometimes separable, sometimes inseparable, and subject in each case to the rules that govern the type of compound formed (17, 30). 

1. The most frequent of these ambiguous prefixes are durch-, über-, um-, unter-, and wieder-. With regard to the prefixes über-, unter-, and wieder-, it may be said that they usually form separable compounds when they are used in a literal sense, and inseparable compounds when they are used in a figurative sense. Concerning durch- and um- no satisfactory generalization can be made. Ordinarily the compound formed by an ambiguous prefix and a given verb is either always separable or always inseparable. 

Occasionally, however, the two types of compound exist side by side; the classic examples of such doublets are übersetzen and wiederholen : ü'bersetzen, setzte ü'ber, ü'bergesetzt (er ſetzt ü'ber) ferry across, row over übersetzen, übersetz'te, übersetzt' (er übersetzt') translate wie'derholen, holte wie'der, wie'dergeholt (er holt wie'der) bring back wiederhoʻlen, wiederhol'te, wiederholt' (er wiederholt') repeat a. The prefixes hinter-, miß-, voll-, and wider- also occur sometimes in separable, sometimes in inseparable, compounds. They are, however, of less frequent occurrence than those given above, and miß- and wider- are quite infrequent as separable prefixes.

15 

[75 75. 

Frequent Uses of the Subjunctive 1. To express possibility: 

Wer könnte das machen? — Herr Kraft wäre der Mann dazu. 

Who could do that? — Mr. Kraft would (possibly) be the man for it. 

2. To express wish, desire, or purpose: 

Wäre der Vater nur da! If only father were here!

Ich gab ihm vier Mark, damit er das Buch kaufe. I gave him four marks so that he might buy the book. 

3. To express seeming reality:

Es war fast, als hätte er jetzt das Ziel ſeiner Reiſe erreicht. It seemed almost as if he had now reached the goal of his journey. 

4. To express oneself in a less positive manner:

Ich möchte den Mann sprechen. I should like to speak with the man. 

5. To express mild command: 

Er komme morgen. Let him come tomorrow. 

6. To express concession: 

Was er auch sage (Wer es auch sage), ich glaube ihm nicht. Whatever he may say (Whoever may say it), I do not believe him. a. In the example under 4 above, the expression of wish or desire is contained in mögen; the subjunctive is used to express the wish in a less positive manner.

EXERCISE B 

1. I (have) heard the doorbell ring, Martha; go to (an) the door, please (bitte). 2. When I came into the room, the lady got up and we shook hands; (3) then she told me to sit down beside her friend. 4. Mr. Walter approached me and gave me his hand. 

5. He asked me why he had seen me so seldom. 6. I answered that I had had to stay at home a long time because I had been sick. 7. I knew well enough that he would not be able to come today, for he caught cold yesterday when it rained. 8. The old man said that he had not been able to help the boy at all because the latter had not cared to work. 9. We are driving out into the country tomorrow, Charles; can you [go] with [us]?

LESSON XVII 

125 

10. I should like to go with you, Fred, but I couldn't [do] it tomorrow because I shall have to work at home in the garden. 

11. If I had only read the card from Father! Then I should know when he will arrive from his journey. 12. I can't do this lesson, Mother; couldn't Mary help me? 13. No, my dear child, you ought to do it yourself. 14. The policeman acted (tat) as if he had not seen what had happened in the street. 15. The two boys have helped each other so that they might go home sooner. 16. The maid stepped into the room and handed the lady a visiting-card. 17. LESSON XVIII The Passive Voice 

1. The various tense and mood forms of a verb in the passive voice are made by adding its past participle to the same tense and mood forms of werden. are 2. The principal parts of lo'ben praise in the passive voice 

Inf.: gelobt werden

Past ind. sing.: ward or wurde gelobt 

Past ind. pl.: wurden gelobt 

Past part.: gelobt worden a. Note that the past participial form of werden as auxiliary of the passive voice is worden, not geworden. 

3. The synopsis of the passive voice of loben in the third person singular is

INDICATIVE 

Present: er wird gelobt

Past: er ward or wurde gelobt 

Present perf.: er ist gelobt worden 

Past perf.: er war gelobt worden 

Future: er wird gelobt werden 

SUBJUNCTIVE 

er werde gelobt er würde gelobt er sei gelobt worden er wäre gelobt worden er werde gelobt werden

Future perf.: er wird gelobt worden sein er werde gelobt worden ſein 

CONDITIONALS 

Present: er würde gelobt werden 

Perfect: er würde gelobt worden sein

IMPERATIVE, SECOND PERSON, IN FULL 

Intimate sing.: werde gelobt Intimate pl.: werdet gelobt 

Formal, sing. and pl.: werden Sie gelobt a. A German gerundive form occurs infrequently: das zu lesende Buch the book that is to be read. b. For the passive of certain intransitive verbs see 79. 126

77] 

LESSON XVIII 

4. Since in English the verb be is used as auxiliary of the passive voice and also as a finite verb, certain difficulties arise when the English-speaking student wishes to express himself in German. When he says, for example,

The doors of the theater were closed at eight o'clock, he may mean that at eight o'clock someone was closing the doors, or he may mean that the doors had been closed before eight o'clock and that he found them already closed at eight o'clock. The German is specific; when he says, Die

Türen wurden um acht Uhr geſchloſſen, he means that someone was then closing them; when he says, Die Türen waren um acht Uhr geschlossen, he means that the action had been completed before eight. If the English-speaking student will visualize his thought, he will have no trouble expressing it correctly in German: he will see someone closing the doors if he means Die Türen wurden um acht Uhr geschlossen; he will see himself facing closed doors if he means Die Türen waren um acht Uhr geſchloſſen. 

77. German Equivalents of the English Passive 

1. The pronoun man one is frequently used as subject of an active verb form where English uses a passive construction:

Um acht Uhr machte man die Türen auf. At eight o'clock the doors were opened. 

2. After the verb sein, and frequently after laſſen, the 

German idiom requires an active infinitive form where the 

English uses the passive: 

Kein Haus war zu sehen. Not a house was to be seen. 

Er ließ es melden. He had it (be) announced.

Als der Vater kam, ließ er den Arzt holen. When Father came he sent for the doctor (had the doctor brought).

[78, 79, 80 

3.

German verbs used reflexively frequently correspond to English passive constructions: 

Da öffnete sich die Tür, und ein alter Mann trat heraus. Then the door was opened, and an old man stepped out. 

Akin to the usage described is the reflexive use of laſſen followed by an active infinitive:

Das läßt sich nicht machen. That can't be done. 

78. Agent, Instrument, and Means with the 

Passive Voice

The agent by whom an act is performed is indicated by bon, the instrument by mit, the means by durch: 

Der Hund wurde von einem Schutzmann durch einen Schlag auf den Kopf getötet. The dog was killed by a policeman by means of a blow on the head.

Der Hund wurde von dem Knaben mit einem Stock geschlagen. 

The dog was hit with a cane by the boy. 

79. Dative Case with Passive Verb Forms 

Verbs that govern the dative in the active voice must be construed with the dative when in the passive: 80. 

Active voice: Der Lehrer riet dem Knaben, nach Hauſe zu gehen. 

The teacher advised the boy to go home.

Passive voice: Dem Knaben wurde vom Lehrer geraten or Es wurde dem Knaben vom Lehrer geraten, nach Hause zu gehen. 

The boy was advised by the teacher to go home.

Comparison of Adjectives 

1. Adjectives are compared by suffixing -er for the comparative and ſt (or est) for the superlative. Some monosyllabics with the stem vowel a, o, or u umlaut this vowel in the comparative and superlative; the most frequent of these adjectives are

81] 

LESSON XVIII 

alt, älter, ältest old arm, ärmer, ärmſt poor jung, jünger, jüngst young falt, fälter, fältest cold frank, fränfer, fränfft sick furz, fürzer, fürzest short lang, länger, längst long stark, stärker, stärkst strong warm, wärmer, wärmst warm 2. Adjectives ending in -e, -el, -en, or -er regularly drop the e of this syllable before the comparative ending -er: teu'er dear, teurer wei'se wise, weiser 3. Adjectives with sibilant endings (-8, ſſ, -ß, =ſch, =t, -3) or with the ending -d or -t (except participial adjectives in -nd) regularly add the ending -eſt for the superlative; other adjectives add only ſt: heiß, heißer, heißest hot schön, schöner, ſchönſt beautiful a. Inflectional endings indicating gender and case of attributive adjectives are the same for comparative and superlative forms as for all other adjectives: eine kleinere Feder a smaller pen die kleinste Feder the smallest pen ein besserer Mantel a better (over)coat der bessere Mantel the better (over)coat die meisten Leute most people, most of the people b. Uninflected superlative forms are exceedingly rare, since superlatives are usually preceded by the definite article. 81. 

Adjectives with Irregular Comparison groß, größer, größt large, tall hoch, höher, höchſt high gut, besser, best good nah(e), näher, nächst near viel, mehr, meist much 1. Hoch changes ch to h between vowels, both in its inflected and its comparative forms: ein hoher Berg a high mountain der höhere Berg the higher mountain

[82, 83 

82. 

Predicate and Adverbial Forms of the Superlative 

1. When used as a predicate adjective or as an adverb, the superlative form is preceded by am, a contraction of an dem, and takes the weak dative ending -en: 

Predicate adjective: Die Tage ſind im Juni am längsten. The days are longest in June.

Adverbial form: Das große Auto fährt am schnellsten. The big automobile goes fastest. a. Distinguish between the use of the superlative in sentences such as

Die Tage sind im Juni am längsten. The days are longest in June.

Diese Tage sind die längsten des Jahres. These days are the longest (ones) of the year.

In the first sentence the superlative is a true predicate adjective; in the second sentence it is really an attributive adjective modifying the noun Tage understood. b. Superlative forms are often reënforced by prefixing aller- to the usual form: 

Dies ist der allerschönste See, den ich kenne. This is the most beautiful lake that I know.

Der See ist zu dieser Jahreszeit am allerschönsten. The lake is most beautiful at this season of the year. 83. 

Comparison of the Adverb gern 

The adverb gern has the irregular comparison gern, lieber, am liebsten: 

Ich lese Gedichte gern. I like to read poems. 

Ich lese lieber Romane. I prefer to (like better to) read novels. 

Ich lese am liebsten Geschichte. I prefer to (like best to) read history.

84] 

LESSON XVIII 

84. 

Absolute Use of the Comparative and Superlative Forms 

1. The comparative form of adjectives is frequently used to indicate a high degree of the quality expressed by the adjective, but no actual comparison:

Eine größere Gesellschaft a company of considerable size Ich wurde längere Zeit aufgehalten. I was detained for some time. 

2. Adverbial phrases of the type aufs -ſte and uninflected superlative forms of adverbs are sometimes used to express a high degree of action with no thought of 

Wir fuhren eiligst ab. We drove off very rapidly. 

Wir haben uns gestern aufs schönste amüsiert. We had a most delightful time yesterday. a. Use of the adverbial forms of the superlative given under 

2 above is a matter of idiom rather than of grammar.

1. Have the automobiles been ordered, Miss Grimm? 2. Yes,

I think they have been ordered for ten o'clock. 3. I had, unfortunately, to stay at home yesterday. 4. I had been told by the doctor to stay at home. 5. Today is the finest day that we have had for (feit dat.) [a] long time. 6. The smallest automobile will soon be left behind by the others, which go much faster. 7. I believe he prefers to buy things for other people rather (eher) than for himself. 22. He would have arrived in Germany a few days sooner if he had sailed on the larger ship. 23. Yes, that is right, perhaps; but he preferred sailing on the smaller steamer because then he would sail with his friends.

LESSON XIX Three Types of Possessive Pronouns 

1. From the possessive adjectives, mein, dein, and so on, are derived three types of possessive pronouns. 

These are

Type 1 Type 2 

Type 3 

mine mei'ner der mei'ne der mei'nige yours intimate sing. dei'ner der dei'ne der dei'nige his ſei'ner der sei'ne der sei'nige hers ih'rer der ih're der ih'rige its ſei'ner der sei'ne der sei'nige ours uns'rer der uns're der uns'rige yours intimate pl. eu'rer der eu're der euʼrige ih'rer der ih're der ih'rige Jh'rer der Jh're der Jh'rige theirs yours formal, sing. and pl. a. The contracted forms given above, unſ'rer, der unſ're, der unſ'rige, eu'rer, der eu're, der euʼrige, are of more frequent occurrence than the uncontracted forms un'ſerer, eu'erer, and so on. 

2. Possessive pronouns of the first group above are inflected like dieser (27, 1):

Dieses Buch ist meines, die da sind deine. This book is mine; those (there) are yours. 

3. Possessive pronouns of the second and third groups are inflected like the definite article followed by an adjective with weak declension:

MASC. 

FEM. 

NEUT. 

NOM. SING. der meine die meine das meine 

GEN. SING. 

des meinen der meinen des meinen

DAT. SING. 

dem meinen der meinen dem meinen

ACC. SING. den meinen die meine das meine etc. etc. etc. 135

[86 

a. A fourth type of possessive pronoun, mein, dein, and so on (except ihr, Jhr), used predicatively, is of less frequent occurrence than those listed above; pronouns of this type refer less specifically to their antecedent than do the others, and mean rather my property, belongs to me:

Ich nahm den Hut, weil er mein war. I took the hat because it belonged to me. b. Like all pronouns, the possessive pronouns agree with their antecedent in gender and number, but not necessarily in case: 

Welcher Hut ist Ihrer (der Ihre, der Ihrige), und welcher ist seiner (der seine, der seinige)? Er hat seinen (den seinen, den feinigen) schon genommen, dieser hier ist meiner (der meine, der meinige). Which hat is yours, and which is his? He has already taken his; this one (here) is mine. 86. 

Substitute Forms for the Dative and 

Accusative Cases of Certain Pronouns 

1. For the dative and accusative cases of the interrogative pronoun was dependent on a preposition the form wo (before vowels mor) is regularly substituted and prefixed to the preposition :

Womit' hat er den Brief geschrieben? With what did he write the letter? 

Woran' denken Sie? What are you thinking about? a. An was, mit was, um was, etc., occur occasionally. The student should permit himself to imitate um was only. 

2. For the dative and accusative cases of the relative pronouns der and welcher dependent on a preposition the form mo (before vowels mor) is commonly, though not always, substituted and prefixed to the preposition when the antecedent of the relative is an inanimate object: 

Das ist die Feder, womit' (mit welcher or mit der) er den Brief geschrieben hat. That is the pen with which he wrote the letter.

87, 88] 137 

LESSON XIX 

3. For the dative and accusative cases of the personal pronouns, and frequently of the demonstrative pronouns der and dieser, dependent on a preposition, the form da (before vowels dar) is regularly substituted and prefixed to the preposition when the antecedent of the personal or demonstrative pronoun is an inanimate object: 

Meine Feder ist verdorben, ich kann nicht damit' schreiben. My pen is ruined; I cannot write with it. a. Notice the shift of accent if the pronominal element of the compound is stressed: 

Da'mit kann ich nicht schreiben. I can't write with that.

EXERCISE B 

1. With whose pen did John write his letter? 2. I do not know whose pen it was, but it was not his. 3. These two sisters are some two or three years older than John. 4. Fred, go into the next room and bring (holen) me my Book of Songs and the letter that is lying on it. 5. The Book of Songs isn't yours, Mary;

1. The form eins is used only in counting, eins, zwei, drei, etc., and in the expressions Es ist eins meaning Es iſt ein Uhr 

It is one o'clock, Es ist halb eins etc. It is half past twelve etc. 

2. When used as a pronoun, ein is inflected like dieſer: 

Fritz hat einen neuen Hut, und ich habe auch einen. Fred has a new hat, and I have one too. 

3. In 16 and 60 the final 8 of sechs is dropped: sechzehn sixteen sechzig sixty 4. All the multiples of ten up to and including 90 have the suffix zig, except 30, which has ßig. 

5. In printing and writing numerals of more than three figures, a space is left between each group, as indicated above, except in printing or writing dates and street numbers: im Jahre 1933 in the year 1933. a. When used as nouns the numerals are feminine: eine Null, die Eins. 141

[91, 92 

Ordinal Numbers 

1. Ordinal numbers are regularly formed by suffixing -te to the cardinals from 2 to 19 and ſte to all others: der zweite the second der zwölfte the twelfth der neunzehnte the nineteenth der zwanzigste the twentieth der einunddreißigste the thirty-first der hundertste the hundredth 

2. The following ordinals are formed irregularly: der erste the first der dritte the third der siebte (or der siebente) the seventh der achte the eighth 3. Sixteenth, sixtieth, seventeenth, and seventieth are derived regularly from the cardinal forms: der sechzehnte der sechzigste der sieb (en) zehnte der sieb (en) zigste a. Being adjectival in their nature, the ordinals are inflected in accordance with the rules of the adjective declension: 92. 

Als zweiter kam Fritz. Fred came second.

Ich habe ihm den zweiten Hut gegeben. I gave him the second hat. 

Expressions of Time 

The following sentences show the essential similarities and differences between German and English usage: 

Wieviel' Uhr ist es? What time is it?

Es ist zehn Uhr. It is ten o'clock. 

Um wieviel' Uhr kommt er an? At what time will he arrive?

Er kommt um elf Uhr an. He will arrive at eleven o'clock. 

Es ist zehn Minuten vor zwölf. 

Es ist zehn Minuten nach eins. 

Es ist ein Viertel zwei. It is a quarter past one. 

Es ist halb zwei. It is half past one. 

It is ten minutes to twelve. 

It is ten minutes after one.

Es ist drei Viertel vier. It is a quarter to four. 

Es ist erst zehn Uhr. It is only ten o'clock.

93] 

LESSON XX 

1. Dreizehn Uhr, vierzehn Uhr, etc. are commonly used instead of ein Uhr, zwei Uhr, etc. in connection with train service after noon. a. It is not incorrect to say Es ist ein Viertel nach eins, Es iſt ein Viertel vor zwei, etc. in expressing the quarter-hours. 93. Dates 

1. The following expressions are used in indicating the day of the month: 

Den wieviel'ten haben wir heute? What date is it today?

Wir haben heute den fünfzehnten Januar. It is January the fifteenth today. 

Es ist heute Montag, der vierte Februar. It is Monday, February the fourth, today. 

Er kam am Montag, dem ersten August, an. He arrived on 

Monday, August the first.

Er kam leßten Dienstag an, den dritten März (adverbial acc.). 

He arrived last Tuesday, March the third. 

Sonntags, Montags, etc. (adverbial gen.). On Sundays, Mondays, etc. 

2. In dating letters one usually writes the date in an abbreviated form of the adverbial accusative: Berlin, den 

2ten Oktober 1933 or Berlin, d. 2. Oft. 1933, which is read, Berlin, den zweiten Oktober neunzehnhundertdreiunddreißig. 

3. Historical dates are usually preceded by the phrase im Jahre:

Schiller wurde im Jahre 1759 geboren, which is read, Schiller wurde im Jahre siebzehnhundertneunundfünfzig geboren or im Jahre tausendſiebenhundertneunundfünfzig. 

Goethe wurde am 28. Auguſt 1749 geboren, which is read,

Goethe wurde am achtundzwanzigsten August siebzehnhundertneunundvierzig geboren or tauſendsiebenhundertneunundvierzig.

EXERCISE A 

1. Als ich heute früh aufstand, war es schon sieben Uhr, und ich mußte mich sehr beeilen, (2) denn um drei Viertel acht muß ich schon in der Schule sein. 3. Ich kleidete mich alſo rasch an und zwanzig Minuten nach sieben war ich im Eßzimmer. 4. Meine 

Tasse Milchkaffee stand schon bereit, daneben mein Butterbrot. 

5. Ich trank eiligst den Kaffee und aß dazu mein Butterbrot (6) und um halb acht stand ich vor der Haustür auf der Straße. 

7. Da ich anderthalb (eineinhalb) Kilometer von der Schule

wohne, mußte ich mich sehr beeilen, (8) aber ich kam richtig an und trat gerade in das Gebäude ein, als Hans und Karl Bleibtreu eintrafen, (9) die doch nur eineinviertel Kilometer entfernt wohnen. 10. Die erſte Stunde heute galt der Geschichte Europas in den letzten Jahren des neunten Jahrhunderts. 11. Glücklicherweise hatte ich aber alle wichtigen Jahreszahlen aus dem 

Leben Karls des Großen gelernt. 12. Also fühlte ich mich ganz ruhig (13) und konnte richtige Antworten geben, als der Lehrer mich befragte. 14. Ich wußte, daß Karl der Große am zweiten

April 742 (oder vielleicht 747) geboren wurde (15) und daß er am achtundzwanzigsten Januar 814 gestorben war; (16) auch daß er sich die Sachsen im Jahre 785 unterworfen hatte (17) und daß er im Jahre 800 in Rom als römischer Kaiser gekrönt wurde. 

18. Der Lehrer schien mit mir recht zufrieden zu sein. 19. Ich war auch mit mir selbst recht zufrieden.

1. Write out in German: 22, 63, 91. 2. Write out in German : 

35, 47, 132. 3. Write out in German: 77, 84, 56. 4. In the year 

1492. 5. In January the days are coldest. 6. In June the days are longest and the nights shortest. 7. On Sunday I always get up late, for on Sundays I do not go to school. 8. You ought (really) to have eaten your buttered roll, Mary, for it lay beside your cup of coffee. 10. My elder brother was born on the twenty-ninth of

February, and so he always says that he was born on March the first. 14. Mary will never tell me how old she is, but I know that she was born on the thirtieth of May, 1906.

APPENDIX A Noun Declension: Summary and Restatement 

In his first approach to the declension of nouns it is necessary for the student to learn the typical forms representative of the four classes of nouns, and the distribution of nouns according to gender and form (number of syllables, endings, and so on) within the various classes. 

Once he is sure of the declensional types, however, he will find that he can classify and use nouns much more readily if he thinks first of their gender and secondly of their distribution in the declensional classes. Classification primarily on the basis of gender will be presented in the summary that follows.

MASCULINE NOUNS General Statement: Masculine Nouns belong to Class II 

Exceptions in Class I. Masculine nouns ending in -el, -en, -er, and one ending in -e, der Käse cheese. 

Exceptions in Class III. Frrtum error, Reichtum riches, and the following monosyllabics: 

Geist spirit 

Mann man 

Gott God, god 

Rand edge 

Wurm worm 

Wald forest 

Leib body

Ort place has the plural form Orte or Örter. 

Exceptions in Class IV. Masculines ending in -e, and the following monosyllabics (not a complete list, but those of frequent occurrence): 147

Christ Christian 

Fürst prince

Graf count Held hero 

Herr gentleman 

Sirt shepherd

Mensch human being 

Narr fool 

Prinz prince

Tor fool 

Exceptions in Class V. Bauer peasant, Muskel muscle, 

Nachbar neighbor, Vetter cousin, and the following with the ending e or -en in the nominative singular (with the nominative singular in -en these are regular nouns of Class I): 

Friede(n) peace 

Funke(n) spark 

Gedanke (n) thought 

Glaube(n) faith 

Haufe(n) heap

Name(n) name 

Same(n) seed 

Schade(n) harm

Nouns ending in -or, and the following monosyllabics of frequent occurrence:

Schmerz pain 

See lake

APPENDIX B The Gender of Nouns 

From the preceding summary of the declension of nouns it is evident that certainty concerning the gender of nouns is of great aid in determining their declension. As a matter of fact, it is not possible to give rules that will always enable the student to recognize the gender of a noun. The following general statements, however, will be found helpful. The careful student will be on the lookout for exceptions of less frequent occurrence than those given in the lists that follow.

GENDER DETERMINED BY MEANING 

Masculine. Names of the days, months, seasons, points of the compass, and mountains.

Feminine. Names of plants, trees, most flowers, many fruits, cardinal numbers, most German rivers, and three countries, die Schweiz Switzerland, die Tschechoslowakei' 

Czechoslovakia, and die Türkei' Turkey.

Neuter. Names of cities and of most countries, letters of the alphabet, and most metals.

GENDER DETERMINED BY ENDING 

Masculine. Nouns of more than one syllable ending in -ich, -ig, -ing, -ling, and -or; most nouns of more than one syllable ending in -el, -en, er; most monosyllabic nouns ending in -f, -ff, -g, -II, -m, -mm, -ng, -pf, =ſch, -ß, and 13. 151

APPENDIX C Uses of the Oblique Cases 

In German the genitive, dative, and accusative cases (oblique cases) are used in a large variety of constructions that are often very different from those of English idiom. 

A very brief outline of the uses of the oblique cases in German is given below. The student who wishes for any purpose to use the language readily will do well to make note especially of verbs and adjectives that govern the genitive or the dative case.

I. USES OF THE GENITIVE CASE 

1. The genitive after verbs. The genitive is used as the sole object after many verbs. The following are fairly typical: bedürfen need entbehren do without gedenken mention, recall harren wait for pflegen care for schonen spare warten wait for 

Ich wollte seiner nicht länger harren. I did not wish to wait for him longer.

The genitive is the secondary object of a verb governing an accusative direct object. The following occur with some frequency: anflagen accuse of berauben deprive of beschuldigen charge with überzeugen convince of versichern assure of würdigen deem worthy of, acknowledge with

Der Mann würdigte seine Frau keines Blickes. The man did not acknowledge his wife with a glance, that is, did not deign to look at his wife. 155

The genitive is the secondary object of a reflexive verb. 

The following are frequent: annehmen take an interest in bedienen make use of bemächtigen get possession of erinnern remember freuen rejoice in rühmen boast of schämen be ashamed of 

Ich erinnere mich seiner nicht. I do not remember him.

Er rühmte sich seiner reichen Freunde, aber er schämte sich der armen. He boasted of his rich friends, but he was ashamed of the poor ones. 

2. The genitive after prepositions. A list of the most frequent prepositions governing the genitive was given in article 87. A much more complete list follows: anstatt or an... ſtatt instead of außerhalb outside (of) diesseit(s) on this side of halber on account of, for the sake of innerhalb within jenseit (8) on the other side of fraft by virtue of mittels by means of trot in spite of, notwithstanding um . . . willen for the sake of ungeachtet notwithstanding unterhalb below, on the lower side of unweit not far from vermittels by means of vermittelst by means of mittelst by means of oberhalb above, beyond ſtatt instead of vermöge by virtue of während during wegen on account of, because of

The following prepositions occur with either the genitive or the dative case: binnen within (binnen eines Jahres or binnen einem Jahre) längs along laut according to ob because of (gen.), above (dat.) trotz in spite of, notwithstanding (gen.), in defiance of (dat.) zufolge according to (dat. is preferable to gen.)

APPENDIX C 

157 3. The genitive after adjectives. A few of the more frequent adjectives governing the genitive are ansichtig in sight of bedürftig in need of bewußt conscious of fähig capable of gewohnt accustomed to habhaft in possession of mächtig in control of, master of müde tired of überdrüssig tired of boll full of würdig worthy of

Arnold war seiner Sinne kaum mächtig. Arnold was scarcely master of (in possession of) his senses. 

4. The genitive with nouns. The use of the genitive in 

German in conjunction with nouns is so nearly identical with English usage that it calls for little comment. Since the English often uses the preposition of with a noun as the equivalent of the German genitive case, the latter is frequently rendered into English by means of this circumlocution: die Liebe einer Mutter a mother's love or the love of a mother ein Glas kalten Wassers a glass of cold water 

5. Adverbial genitive. Various adverbial relationships are expressed by the genitive—most frequently, time, place, and manner : 

Time: eines Tages one day des Abends of an evening, evenings

Place: Ein alter Mann kam des Weges. An old man came along the road.

Manner: Romm ins Haus und ruhe dich aus", rief er leuch= tenden Auges. "Come into the house and rest," he cried, his eyes shining (with shining eyes).

II. USES OF THE DATIVE CASE 

1. The dative after verbs. The dative is used as the sole object after many verbs. The following are of frequent occurrence: antworten answer begegnen meet danken thank dienen serve folgen follow grauen be afraid gefallen please helfen help gehorchen obey raten advise gelingen succeed trauen trust 

Antworte mir. Answer me. 

Ich bin ihm gefolgt. I followed him.

Es gelang mir nicht, das zu tun. I did not succeed in doing that. 

Es graute mir or Mir graute (es). I felt afraid, was horrified. 

Verbs compounded with certain prefixes regularly govern the dative; especially frequent are the prefixes ab-, an-, auf-, aus-, bei-, ein-, ent-, entgegen-, nach-, vor-, wider-, zu-: 

Es fiel mir auf. It attracted my attention, It struck me, I noticed.

Erich ging seinem Freunde entgegen. Erich went to meet his friend. 

Es kam mir vor. It seemed to me.

The dative is one of two objects of a verb. In such cases the dative is usually a dative of indirect object, indicating the person to whom something is given, or a dative of separation, indicating the person from whom something is taken:

Mein Bruder hat mir das Buch gegeben. My brother gave me the book. 

Der Mann hat dem Bauer das Pferd gestohlen. The man stole the horse from the peasant.

Of less frequent occurrence are verbs that govern a dative reflexive object of the person and an accusative object of the thing:

APPENDIX C 

159 Er hat sich das Bild lange angesehen. He looked at the picture for a long time.

Ich betrachtete mir das Haus genau. I looked at the house carefully. 

Überlege dir die Sache bis morgen.

Consider the matter till tomorrow. 

2. The dative after prepositions. A list of the most common prepositions governing the dative was given in article 88. The following list is more complete: aus out of, from außer beside (s), except nach to, toward, after, according to bei at the house of, with, near, nächst next by, at entgegen toward gegenüber opposite nebst along with ſeit since samt together with, along with gemäß in accordance with, acvon from, of, by cording to zu to, toward, at, for mit with, in company with, zuwider contrary to along with

Entgegen, gegenüber, gemäß, nach, zu, and zuwider may all follow the noun or pronoun that they modify; entgegen, gegenüber, gemäß, and zuwider almost always follow. 

3. The dative after adjectives. The list of adjectives that govern the dative is a long one. The following are of frequent occurrence: ähnlich similar angenehm pleasant getreu faithful nützlich useful gewachsen equal to peinlich painful bange afraid gewogen gracious schuldig indebted behilflich helpful gleich equal teuer precious befannt known leid painful dankbar thankful eigen peculiar lieb dear feind hostile fremd strange nahe near nötig necessary treu faithful überlegen superior berhaßt hated verwandt related

Any adjective preceded by zu or allzu or followed by genug may govern the dative. 

4. Dative of reference, or concern. The dative case is often used where English idiom requires a possessive genitive or a possessive adjective:

Das Kind warf sich der Mutter in die Arme. The child threw itself into its mother's arms.

Das Kind warf sich mir in die Arme. The child threw itself into my arms.

Infrequently a dative of reference and a possessive adjective occur in the same sentence:

Ich habe mir meinen Fuß verrenft. I have sprained my ankle. 

The dative is frequently used to indicate the person for whose advantage or disadvantage something is done: 

Er zeichnet mich in schwarzer Kreide, ich habe ihm dreimal ſizen müſſen. He is sketching me in black crayon; I have had to sit for him three times.

III. USES OF THE ACCUSATIVE CASE 

1. The accusative after verbs. The accusative is used as the sole object of a transitive verb, as in English : 

Ich habe den alten Mann gestern gesehen. I saw the old man yesterday. 

A few verbs govern two accusative objects:

Der Vater lehrte seine Kinder Deutsch. The father taught his children German.

For verbs governing both an accusative and a genitive object see page 155.

For verbs governing both an accusative and a dative object see page 158.

APPENDIX C 

161 

2. The accusative after prepositions. A practically complete list of the most frequent prepositions governing the accusative only was given in Article 89. To these may be added gen, which occasionally occurs in place of gegen, and the rather uncommon ſonder without, beyond, as ſonder 

Zweifel without doubt, beyond any doubt.

The list of prepositions governing either the dative or the accusative was given in full in Article 33. To the detailed rules given in Article 33, 1, 2, 3, certain slight restrictions should be noted, since the German attaches to some verbs certain meanings that are not readily felt by the foreigner. After anfommen and einfehren and a preposition the dative case is used:

Wir kamen um zehn Uhr in der Stadt an. We arrived in the city at ten o'clock.

Wir fehrten in einem alten Wirtshaus ein. We went into (put up at) an old inn.

After hüllen and fleiden followed by in the accusative is regularly used:

Das Mädchen war in flägliche Lumpen gehüllt or gekleidet. 

The girl was dressed in wretched rags.

After ſich ſetzen an accusative is frequently used with the meaning go to a place and seat oneself:

Er setzte sich unter den Baum. He went under the tree and sat down. 

3. The accusative after adjectives. A limited number of adjectives are commonly followed by the accusative, though originally the genitive case was used; this modern use of the accusative is due to a confusion of the genitive and accusative cases of the pronoun es. The most frequent of these adjectives are

gewahr aware of fatt tired of, satiated with gewohnt accustomed to überdrüffig satiated with zufrieden satisfied with los rid of müde tired of

Reinhard konnte sie nicht gewahr werden. Reinhard could not see her, catch sight of her. 

4. Adverbial accusative. The accusative case is used to express a great variety of adverbial relationships: 

Duration of time: drei Tage lang (for) three days

Extent of space: drei Kilometer weit a distance of three kilometers

Weight: Es wiegt zwei Zentner. It weighs two hundred pounds. 

Height or depth: zehn Fuß hoch, tief ten feet high, deep 

Ground gone over is regularly expressed by the adverbial accusative:

Er ging denselben Weg zurück, den er gekommen war. He returned along (by) the same road that he had come (over). 

Definite time when is expressed by the adverbial accusative: 

Noch diesen Abend werden Sie ihn sehen. You shall see him this very evening. 

5. Accusative absolute. The accusative is used with a past participle or in connection with a prepositional phrase.

Almost invariably a portion of the body is named in descriptions made by means of the accusative absolute. 

Da stand er, die Arme über der Brust gefreuzt. There he stood, (with) his arms crossed over his breast.

Er wanderte den Weg (adv. acc.) entlang, den Tornister auf dem 

Rücken, den Stab in der Hand. He walked along the road, (with) his knapsack on his back, his staff in his hand.

ORAL EXERCISES 

In the use of the following exercises, both teacher and student are at times tacitly asked to accept the fiction that the teacher is questioning the student concerning a personal experience and that the student is speaking of this experience, as in the exercises of Lessons II and VI and in other exercises. The acceptance of this fiction makes possible to a reasonable degree the use of pronoun and verb forms of the second person. In many classes the teacher will undoubtedly wish to discard entirely the forms du and ihr in the second person, substituting the Sie commensurate with the students' years. 

LESSON I 

1. Wo sitt Karl? 2. Wer kommt ins Zimmer? 3. Was fragt Fräulein Schmidt? 4. Wo ist Karls Vater? 5. Wer ist mit den Mädchen im Garten? 6. Sind Karls Brüder im Zimmer oder im Garten? 7. Mit wem kommt die Mutter jetzt aus dem 

Garten? 8. Was sagt die Mutter zu Fräulein Schmidt? 9. Was antwortet Fräulein Schmidt? 10. Wohin geht die Mutter mit 

Fräulein Schmidt? 11. Zu wem gehen jetzt die Mädchen?

Zimmer? 15. Geht Karl jetzt aus dem Garten oder in den 

Garten? 16. Wohin gehen jetzt die Mädchen? 17. Mit wem sind die Töchter jetzt in dem Garten? 18. Mit wem ist Karls 

Vater in dem Garten? 19. Wo sind jetzt Kellers? 20. Wer ist mit Kellers im Garten?

Ihren Herrn Vater schreiben, oder an Ihre Frau Mutter? 

25. Wann hoffen Sie wieder zu Hause zu ſein? 

1. dazu to that. 2. ankommen arrive.

LESSON XVII 

1. Bei wem ist Frau Klein? 2. Was machen die beiden 

Damen? 3. Was sagte nun Frau Krüger zu ihrer Freundin? 

4. Was schien die Frau Klein mit sich ins Haus zu bringen? 

5. Was sagte Frau Krüger von dem letzten Winter? 6. Was hatte Frau Krüger während des letzten Winters nur selten machen. fönnen? 7. Erklären Sie mir, warum Frau Krüger sagte, daß ſie ſo viel durchgemacht habe. 8. Erklären Sie mir, warum Frau 

Krüger ihre Kinder und ihren Mann hatte selbst pflegen müſſen.¹ 

9. Mochte Frau Krüger ihre Nachbarin, die über ihr wohnte? 

10. Was sagte sie von den Kindern der Familie Walter? 11. Was sagte sie von ihrem Manne? 12. Was sagte sie von sich selbst und der Frau Walter? 13. Was für eine Person scheint die Frau

Walter zu sein? 14. Was geschieht nun, indem Frau Krüger

von der Frau Walter spricht? 15. Wer tritt gleich darauf ins 

Zimmer? 16. Was übergibt das Dienstmädchen der Frau 

Krüger? 17. Was sagt Frau Krüger zum Dienstmädchen? 

18. Was sagt sie zu ihrer Freundin? 19. Was macht Frau

Krüger, als ihre Nachbarin von oben ins Zimmer eintritt? 

20. Was machen die beiden Damen? 21. Was sagte nun die Frau

Krüger zu Frau Walter? 22. Wie nannte Frau Krüger die Frau 

Walter? 23. Hatte Frau Krüger viel Gutes von Frau Walter gesagt, als sie von ihr sprach? 24. Scheint die Frau Krüger sich zu freuen, als sich ihre Nachbarin wieder bei ihr sehen läßt? 

1. hatte selbst pflegen müſſen, a slightly modified transposed order frequently used with the perfect tenses of modal auxiliaries. 

LESSON XVIII 

1. Guten Morgen, Fräulein Schubert, ich habe Sie gestern nicht zu Hause finden können; wo waren Sie? 2. Haben Sie einen schönen Tag erlebt, Fräulein Schubert? 3. Wie famen Sie nach dem See? 4. Warum hatte man mehrere Autos bestellt? 

5. Für wieviel Uhr hatte man die Autos beſtellt? 6. Warum sind Sie selber nicht mit den andern gefahren? 7. Welches Auto ließ man für Sie und Ihre Freundin Fräulein Grimm zurück? 

8. Warum ließ man gerade das größte Auto für nur zwei Personen zurück? 9. Wer kam nun endlich? 10. Was machten Sie, als

Fräulein Grimm endlich kam? 11. Was machten eben die anderen, als Sie am See ankamen? 12. Wo sahen Sie mehrere kleine 

Schiffe? 13. Wohin sollten die Körbe mit dem Mittagessen gebracht werden? 14. Von wem sollte die Gesellschaft übergeſetzt werden? 15. Wie wollte man vom Königssee nach dem Oberſee kommen? 16. Von wem sind die Körbe die kurze Strecke nach dem Obersee getragen worden? 17. Wie haben Sie sich nun in der schönen Gegend unterhalten¹? 18. Wohin gingen Sie nun,

LESSON XIX 

1. Wie heißt Ihr jüngerer Bruder, Fräulein Schulze? 2. Wie alt ist der Junge? 3. Zeigen Sie nach dem Tische, bitte. 4. Sagen 

Sie mir, bitte, was darauf liegt. 5. Wer hat den Brief darauf gelegt? 6. Ist der Brief gut oder schlecht geschrieben? 7. Was fragen Sie nun den Bruder? 8. Wie antwortet Hans und was macht er dabei¹? 9. Was hatten Sie dem Jungen doch gesagt? 

10. Warum sollte der Junge mit seiner eigenen Feder schreiben, und nicht mit Ihrer? 11. War es Hans, der seine Feder verdorben hatte? 12. Wer hatte sie denn verdorben? 13. Hat Hans jetzt gar keine Feder mehr, womit er einen ordentlichen Brief ſchreiben fann? 14. Was hätte der Hans seinem kleinen Bruder sagen sollen? 15. Warum hat es nichts geholfen, daß Hans das seinem 

Bruder gesagt hat? 16. Was macht der kleine Bruder, sobald 

Hans nicht mehr im Zimmer ist? 17. Was macht der Hans, wenn er sieht, daß der kleine Bruder seine Feder verdirbt? 

18. Was aber sagen Sie, wenn der Hans den kleinen Bruder schlägt? 19. Ist es wahr, daß Sie dem Hans sagen, er ſolle

Ihre Feder liegen lassen, wenn er die Hand daran legt? Darf er mit der Ihrigen nicht schreiben? 20. Was macht Hans jezt? 

21. Warum läuft er aus dem Zimmer? 22. Tut es Ihnen nicht leid um² den armen Jungen? 

1. dabei meanwhile, as he speaks. 2. um for.

LESSON XX 

1. Den wievielten haben wir heute? 2. Um wieviel Uhr sind

Gebäude ein, als Sie eintrafen? 14. Wohnen Sie der Schule etwa¹ näher als Bleibtreus, oder nicht so nah? 15. Was hatten

EXERCISES FOR REVIEW LESSONS I-IV 

1. My friend Fred did not come into the garden; he remained in the room with your friends. 2. The teacher of the class was sitting at the head of the table, and the children were sitting around the table. 3. Where are you, Charles? 4. I am sitting under a tree in the garden. 5. I am waiting here for Fred; he is coming to [see] me today. 6. Have your friends not yet come, Charles? 7. No, Miss Keller; Fred is still at school, and his brothers are still in the city. 8. The room is small, but very attractive; it has three windows. 9. Did you find the family at home, Miss Smith? 10. No, I did not find them. 11. I spoke a few words with their friend Mr. Keller. 12. He said, "The family took the train at nine o'clock for the village." 13. The boys were working with the men in the fields or in the forests, but the girls were staying in the villages with their mothers. 

14. The gardens in our city are now very beautiful. 15. Today we read several poems of Goethe and Schiller. 16. The teacher explained Schiller's poem "Hope" to us. 17. Our sisters have already gone home with Mother; our brothers are waiting at the station for a friend. 18. Finally I found the road, and then we soon (bald) reached the dwelling-house. 19. The roads led us through several villages; here the children were playing before the houses, and the men were working in the gardens. 20. I asked, "Is Mrs. Werner at home today?" and the girl answered, "She is in the country today, but you [will] find her at home this evening.¹" 

1. this evening heute abend.

LESSONS V-VIII 

William came 

1. While I was copying my lesson for tomorrow, into the room and said, "Charles, two automobiles have collided in the street." 2. The old man was so poor that he had no 179

money to buy a good overcoat. 3. What are you holding there in your hand, Elizabeth? Oh, that is a postal card that has come from America. 4. With the money that their uncle had given them the girls had bought new hats and silk stockings. 

5. Our brothers had bought books in which [there] were poems of Goethe, Schiller, and Heine. 6. Father, have you seen the new fountain pen that Aunt Lotty gave me as a birthday present? 

7. The presents that Mary bought for her little brother cost so much that she has not a penny left. 8. If you get up from the chair and look out the window, you [will] see what is happening in front of the house. 9. The policeman who came up questioned the people who were standing in the street. 10. "What is happening in the street?" my little brother called to me. 

11. "Run down the steps and see what the noise is,” I answered him. 12. Do not forget that you still have a few lessons to prepare for tomorrow, Mary, and come home soon (bald) again. 

13. Do you see the new light coat that I have bought, Fred? 

Oh, yes, it looks very nice; but let me read. 14. Next month spring begins, and then you [will] not wear your dark coat any more. 15. Our little brother stands at the door and looks after us when we go to school; the little [fellow] calls, "Good-by, good-by!" 16. Your new automobile is not so large as the old one, I think, Mrs. Werner. —No, it is not so large, but it was not so expensive either. 17. What are you going to do now, William? I am going home to prepare my lessons for tomorrow. 18. What is the old man who is going across the street yonder carrying in his hand?—I think he is carrying a book in each hand; he sits in the big garden every day and reads. 19. As 

I was going out the door, two automobiles collided in the street. 

20. With whom is Mother talking at the door? It is the old woman whom we saw yesterday in the little village in the country. 

1. to und.

LESSONS IX-XII 

1. Are those the same old gentlemen with whom your brother was talking this morning, Doctor? 2. Yes, Mrs. Keller, they are the ones of whom your sister told us yesterday. 3. Did

181 you know that Professor Werner's two sons had studied at the University of Leipzig? 4. Yes, but they are said to have studied at several German (deutſch-) universities. 5. I was very sorry that your daughters could not be at [the] Smiths', Mrs. (Professor) 

6. They were also very sorry, Doctor -; but they had caught cold a few days ago, and so they couldn't dance last evening. 7. It has been raining for several days, and the children have been very much vexed that they were not allowed to drive into the country to dance in one of the villages. 8. Sit down on the big chair, Fred; I shall tell Charles that you are waiting for him here. 9. My brother was quite ill yesterday, but he is feeling much better today. 10. Who are the two gentlemen yonder? - They are two famous physicians from America. 

11. The one in the light clothes is Doctor Weiss, whose book on diseases of the heart is very well known. 12. The one who has such blue eyes is said to be [a] professor of diseases of the ear at a big university in America. 13. You must not go to school today, Charles, for you caught cold yesterday. 14. Open the window, William; it has become very warm in the room. I think it may rain any (jeden) moment. 15. When I bowed to Miss 

Keller and asked her for the next dance, she answered, "With pleasure"; and it was a pleasure to dance with her, too—she dances so well. 16. What kind of (a) coat shall you buy this spring, Mary? 17. I shall buy a long, light coat; for the coats are said to be very long and light-colored this spring. 18. With whose automobile shall we drive into the country tomorrow? 

19. I shall drive with William Keller, and you shall drive with

Mary Ludwig, whose brother is now at home again. 20. Those are the girls with whom your brothers danced last evening, are they not?

LESSONS XIII-XVI 

1. I did not care to talk with him, for he is one of those persons who always claim to know everything. 2. Today we shall probably have to stay at home if it does not soon stop raining. 

3. I was just going to drive to the station in order to set out on my journey to Germany; but then my mother became sick, and

I had to wait till she was better. 4. These old peasants do not care to use machines; they want to do everything with their hands. 5. After I had danced with Miss Ludwig for some time, we sat down by the open window and continued our former conversation. 6. You cannot help the people in this way. 7. We were thinking about a trip to Germany this spring; but our house has burned down, and now it is no longer possible to think about a trip. 8. I went (wenden) to my brother for advice; he sent me with a letter to his wife's uncle, and the latter helped me. 9. My name is William; my father named me after his brother. 10. Do you know Mr. Guttmann, the banker? 11. No,

I do not know him, but I became acquainted with one of his brothers when I was studying at the university. 12. I should have been very much pleased to become acquainted with your friends (fem.). 13. If you had sent the letter to me by one of your sons, I should certainly have got it yesterday. 14. If you had come home at once when it began to rain, you would not have caught cold. 15. If I thought as you [do], Mrs. 

I should let my children play with those of my neighbors. 

16. Should you ask the professor's wife for advice, if you did not know her very well? 17. John told me a few days ago that his brother William is still studying at the University of Berlin. 

18. In the letter that my sister got this morning from Louise

Berger, the latter wrote that her aunt had set out on her journey yesterday. 19. The doctor says that his daughters are still in the West, but that they will soon be at home again. 20. I had heard that your brothers had been studying at the University of Munich, but today your father told me that they were still in Berlin.

LESSONS XVII-XX 

1. Mrs. Krüger said that she had not been able to go out for (ſeit) several weeks, because her husband had been very sick.

183 because the mother has been sick and has not been able to care for the children herself. 5. Father would not have been able to call on our new neighbors even if he had wanted to. 6. Father says we are to take the biggest automobile, because it goes fastest, and that is his. 17. Can you tell us, sir, how far it is to the shore of the nearest lake? 18. By (bis) a quarter past twelve we shall be there; we can eat dinner by the shore of the lake and then look about in the neighborhood. 20. What date is it today, Fred? 1. Let's drive fahren wir.

LESSONS XII, XIII, XV, XVI, XVII 

1. Could you drive out into the country with us tomorrow, Mrs. Schubert, you and your family? 2. We should like to visit friends who live in the vicinity of the village of Rosenberg. 

3. They told us to bring a company of considerable size, about twenty to (bis) twenty-five persons. 4. We should have to leave at nine o'clock at latest; for from Rosenberg we are to drive to a

pretty little lake that lies in the vicinity. 5. You must not bring dinner with you, either. 6. Mrs. Werner that is our friend's name says that she intends to look after (machen) all that herself. 7. I am very sorry that we shall not be able to be with your friends tomorrow. 8. If you had only been able to tell me (that) sooner (früher), we should certainly have been glad to have the outing. 9. Now it is too late, I am sorry to say. 10. A few days ago my husband received a letter in which friends wrote to him that they would be in (the) town on Wednesday, the thirtieth. 11. Of course they wished to visit us. 12. My husband wrote them we should be very glad to see them again, and he hoped they would be able to stay in (the) town overnight¹; we could easily lodge them with us. 13. They are the Kellers, from Dresden; I think you have already become acquainted with them at our house. 14. Certainly, certainly; they are very charming people. 15. But now you must surely [go] with [us], and the Kellers are to [come] with [us], too. 16. We shall have a pleasant day together. 

1. die Nacht über.

VOCABULARIES

Explanations ADJECTIVES AND ADVERBS 

Since most adjectives can be used as adverbs in German, adverbial forms are given only when they differ from those of the adjectives. 

NOUNS 

The gender of nouns is shown by the article preceding them. The nominative singular, genitive singular, and nominative plural of all nouns are given; the nominative plural is omitted when the form is not in use.

PREPOSITIONS 

The case or cases governed by prepositions are indicated by gen., dat., acc., or dat. and acc. When a preposition that may govern either the dative or the accusative case occurs in a phrase in which it governs only one case, the case governed is indicated: denken an acc. think of. VERBS The principal parts of all verbs occurring in the lessons are given, followed in parentheses by the form of the third person singular present indicative active when this form shows any irregularity and in the case of separable compound verbs. Verbs whose perfect tenses are formed by ſein have ist before the past participle. The infinitive form of verbs that may be used reflexively is followed by (sich). Verbs that govern the genitive or the dative case are followed by gen. or dat.; in the case of verbs governing the dative of the person and the accusative of the thing, the fact is indicated by dat. pers.: glauben, glaubte, geglaubt dat. pers. believe.

LENGTH OF VOWELS AND ACCENTS 

Long or short vowels that are frequently mispronounced by students have the length indicated. Compound words are frequently marked with two accents. In such cases the heavier accent indicates the main stress.

Reference numbers in heavy-faced type after words indicate paragraphs in the text. Roman numbers after proper nouns indicate the lesson vocabularies in which the words are defined. 192
